---
type: Provision
title: "Family Code, Article 36: Psychological Incapacity"
description: "Declares void from the beginning any marriage contracted by a party who at the time of celebration was psychologically incapacitated to comply with the essential marital obligations, even if such incapacity becomes manifest only after solemnization."
enactment: "The Family Code of the Philippines (Executive Order No. 209)"
provision: "Art. 36"
tags:
  - civil-law
  - family-code
  - marriage
  - void-marriages
  - psychological-incapacity
  - tan-andal-doctrine
  - personality-structure
  - juridical-antecedence
  - legal-incurability
  - clear-and-convincing-evidence
status: stable
review_status: reviewed
publication_approved: true

> Art. 36. A marriage contracted by any party who, at the time of the celebration, was psychologically incapacitated to comply with the essential marital obligations of marriage, shall likewise be void even if such incapacity becomes manifest only after its solemnization. (As amended by Executive Order 227)[^family-code]

# Explanation

Article 36 of Executive Order No. 209 (the Family Code of the Philippines), as amended by Executive Order No. 227, declares void *ab initio* any marriage contracted by a party who, at the time of its celebration, was psychologically incapacitated to comply with the essential marital obligations of marriage, even if such incapacity becomes manifest only after solemnization.[^2] [^3] [^4]  Positioned within Chapter 3 (Void and Voidable Marriages) of Title I (Marriage) of the Family Code, Article 36 operates as a sui generis ground for the judicial declaration of absolute nullity of marriage.[^5] [^6]  Unlike defects in formal or essential requisites under Articles 35, 37, and 38, or vices of consent in voidable marriages under Article 45, psychological incapacity does not presuppose the absence of a marriage license, an incestuous or bigamous impediment, or a vitiation of consent through fraud, force, intimidation, or physical impotence.[^7] [^8] [^9] [^10]   [^11]  Instead, it addresses a deeply ingrained maladaptive personality structure existing at the moment of the wedding that renders a spouse genuinely unable (rather than merely unwilling, neglectful, or ill-equipped) to understand, assume, and discharge the fundamental covenantal duties of marriage and parenthood.[^12] [^13] [^14] 

Over more than three decades of Supreme Court jurisprudence, the doctrinal architecture of Article 36 has evolved through three distinct eras: first, the foundational formulation in [*Santos v. Court of Appeals*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) (1995), which crystallized the twin (and later tripartite) requisites of gravity, juridical antecedence, and incurability;[^15]  second, the rigid, clinical, eight-point guideline regime imposed by [*Republic v. Court of Appeals and Molina*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/34463) (1997), which for twenty-four years treated psychological incapacity as a medical or psychiatric illness requiring a clinical root-cause diagnosis;[^16] [^17]  and third, the landmark *En Banc* paradigm shift in [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), which expressly abandoned the pathological framing of *Molina*, reconceptualized psychological incapacity as a purely legal precept grounded in personality structure and interpersonal incompatibility, dispensed with mandatory psychiatric or clinical examinations, recalibrated incurability from a medical prognosis to an enduring legal and relational reality, and established *clear and convincing evidence* as the governing quantum of proof to overcome the presumption of marital validity.[^18] [^19] 

---

## 1. Statutory Text, Legislative Genesis, and Canon Law Roots

### 1.1 Textual Structure and the Executive Order No. 227 Amendment

As originally promulgated under Executive Order No. 209 on July 6, 1987, and amended eleven days later by Executive Order No. 227 on July 17, 1987, prior to the Family Code's effectivity on August 3, 1988, Article 36 consists of a single sentence containing four operative legal propositions:[^20]  [^21] 

1. **Any-party standing and unilateral locus:** The marriage is contracted by any party who suffers from psychological incapacity, meaning the incapacity may reside in either the husband, the wife, or both spouses concurrently;[^22] [^23] [^24] 
2. **Temporal anchor at celebration:** The incapacity must exist at the time of the celebration of the marriage, establishing the statutory requirement of juridical antecedence;[^25] [^26] [^27] 
3. **Functional nexus to essential marital obligations:** The condition must render the afflicted party psychologically incapacitated to comply with the essential marital obligations of marriage, anchoring the inquiry not in abstract mental health labels, but in functional behavioral inability vis-à-vis spousal and parental duties;[^28]   [^29]  and
4. **Post-solemnization manifestation:** The marriage is void even if such incapacity becomes manifest only after its solemnization, recognizing that latent personality structures frequently surface in overt dysfunctional behavior only when confronted with the concrete stresses, intimacies, and responsibilities of married life.[^30] [^31] [^32] 

The history of prescription must be distinguished from retroactivity. The current Family Code text records that Republic Act No. 8533 deleted the ten-year limitation for marriages celebrated before the Code took effect; it does not record a separate period running from manifestation in post-Code marriages.[^33] Article 256 provides:

Art. 256. This Code shall have retroactive effect insofar as it does not prejudice or impair vested or acquired rights in accordance with the Civil Code or other laws.[^34]

### 1.2 Deliberations of the Family Code Revision Committee

In [*Santos v. Court of Appeals*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) (1995), [*Republic v. Court of Appeals and Molina*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/34463) (1997), [*Ngo Te v. Yu-Te*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/48851) (2009), and [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), the Supreme Court extensively reconstructed the deliberations of the Civil Code and Family Law Revision Committee chaired by Justice Jose B.L. Reyes and Justice Ricardo C. Puno, with Justice Eduardo P. Caguioa, Dean Fortunato Gupit, Jr., Prof. Flerida Ruth P. Romero, Dean Bartolome S. Carale, and Prof. Esteban B. Bautista as members, and Archbishop Oscar V. Cruz and Rev. Fr. Gerardo Veloso as canon law consultants.[^35] [^36] [^37]  Three deliberate drafting choices by the Committee permanently shaped the interpretation of Article 36:

* **Rejection of absolute divorce and post-marriage supervening grounds:** The Committee consciously avoided introducing absolute divorce into the Family Code, seeking instead a middle ground that could provide relief to spouses trapped in loveless, meaningless unions that were effectively dead from the very beginning without offending executive or ecclesiastical sensibilities.[^38] [^39]  The Committee initially considered including spouses who were not psychologically capacitated at the time of the celebration *or subsequently became* incapacitated, which would have operated as a functional divorce for supervening breakdowns.[^40]  After debate, the Committee restricted Article 36 strictly to incapacity existing *at the time of the celebration* of marriage, making it a ground for nullity *ab initio* rather than dissolution of a valid marriage.[^41] [^42] 
* **Distinction between psychological incapacity and insanity (vice of consent):** During the Committee debates, Dean Carale and Justice Caguioa addressed whether psychological incapacity was merely redundant with insanity under voidable marriages (Article 45(2)).[^43] [^44] [^45]  Justice Caguioa clarified that psychological incapacity is fundamentally distinct from mental incapacity or insanity: mental incapacity is a *vice of consent* relating to the cognitive ability to understand the marriage ceremony, and is potentially curable through lucid intervals or psychiatric treatment (hence rendering a marriage merely voidable and subject to ratification by cohabitation), whereas psychological incapacity refers to the inability to *comply with the essential obligations* of marriage even if the party possesses normal intelligence and cognitive awareness during the wedding.[^46] [^47] [^48] [^49] 
* **Refusal to define the term or enumerate illustrative examples:** The Committee deliberately refused both to define psychological incapacity in the text of the Code and to enumerate specific behavioral or clinical examples.[^50] [^51] [^52]  Citing the canon law principle *ne quid nimis* (not too much), the codifiers feared that a statutory definition or checklist of examples would trigger the maxim *exclusio unius est exclusio alterius*, straitjacketing trial judges and excluding equally meritorious manifestations of incapacity.[^53] [^54]  They intended Article 36 to remain resilient, elastic, and responsive to evolving factual contexts through case-by-case judicial application.[^55] [^56] 

### 1.3 Canon 1095 of the 1983 New Code of Canon Law

Article 36 was expressly lifted from and modeled upon Paragraph 3 of Canon 1095 of the 1983 *Codex Iuris Canonici* (New Code of Canon Law) promulgated by Pope John Paul II.[^57] [^58] [^59]  Canon 1095 declares who are incapable of contracting marriage under Catholic ecclesiastical law:

1. Those who lack the sufficient use of reason (*qui sufficienti rationis usu carent*);
2. Those who suffer from a grave discretion of judgment concerning the essential matrimonial rights and duties mutually to be handed over and accepted (*qui laborant gravi defectu discretionis iudicii circa iura et officia matrimonialia essentialia mutuo tradenda et acceptanda*); and
3. Those who are not able to assume the essential obligations of marriage for causes of a psychic nature (*qui ob causas naturae psychicae obligationes matrimonii essentiales assumere non valent*).[^60] [^61] [^62] 

Canon 1095(3), rooted in the Roman law maxim *impossibilium nulla obligatio est* (no one can be obligated to perform the impossible), recognizes that marriage is not merely a momentary exchange of wedding vows (*matrimonium in fieri*), but a lifelong interpersonal consortium (*matrimonium in facto esse*) requiring the psychological capacity to deliver the object of the marital consent.[^63] [^64]  A person whose personality structure prevents the assumption of the essential obligations of conjugal life cannot validly give matrimonial consent, because one cannot validly bind oneself to what one is psychologically incapable of fulfilling.[^65] [^66] 

In [*Republic v. Court of Appeals and Molina*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/34463) (1997), Guideline No. 7 initially instructed civil courts that interpretations given by the National Appellate Matrimonial Tribunal of the Catholic Church in the Philippines, while not controlling or decisive, should be given great respect by civil courts because Article 36 was derived from Canon 1095.[^67] [^68]  In [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021) and [*Tilar v. Republic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/63283) (2017), the Supreme Court refined the constitutional boundaries of this borrowing: while Canon 1095 illuminates the historical origin of Article 36, the Philippines is a secular constitutional democracy governed by the inviolable separation of Church and State under Article II, Section 6 of the 1987 Constitution.[^const-1987] [^70] [^71]  Civil marriage is a civil institution regulated exclusively by the Family Code, and civil courts retain sole jurisdiction to determine the validity of marriages under civil law.[^72] [^73]  Consequently, canonical annulment decrees issued by Catholic matrimonial tribunals remain persuasive evidentiary pieces when supported by factual findings that independently satisfy civil law standards, as in [*Antonio v. Reyes*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/40916) (2006) and [*Camacho-Reyes v. Reyes*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/54276) (2010), but they neither bind civil courts nor substitute for an independent judicial evaluation under Article 36, as emphasized in [*Republic v. Amata*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68626) (2022) and [*Republic v. Pangasinan*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/62277) (2016).[^74] [^75] [^76] [^77] 

---

## 2. Constitutional Framework: Marriage, Family, and Secular Nullity

### 2.1 Article XV of the 1987 Constitution and the Presumption of Validity

The interpretation of Article 36 has always operated within the constitutional mandate of Article XV of the 1987 Constitution.[^const-1987]  Section 1 declares that the State recognizes the Filipino family as the foundation of the nation and shall strengthen its solidarity and actively promote its total development.[^const-1987]  Section 2 proclaims that marriage, as an inviolable social institution, is the foundation of the family and shall be protected by the State.[^const-1987]  Echoing this constitutional command, Article 1 of the Family Code defines marriage as a special contract of permanent union between a man and a woman entered into in accordance with law for the establishment of conjugal and family life, which is the foundation of the family and an inviolable social institution whose nature, consequences, and incidents are governed by law and not subject to stipulation.[^78] 

From this constitutional and statutory architecture flows the **presumption of validity of marriage** (*semper praesumitur pro matrimonio*).[^79] [^80] [^81]  He who asserts the nullity of a marriage bears the burden of proving that an impediment or psychological incapacity existed at the time of the wedding.[^82] [^83] [^84]  Because the State is an indispensable third party to every marital contract, judgment on the pleadings, summary judgment, confession of judgment, or stipulation of facts declaring a marriage void is strictly prohibited, and the Office of the Solicitor General (OSG) along with the public prosecutor participates to guard against collusion and fabricated evidence.[^85] [^86] [^87] [^88] 

### 2.2 The *Kalaw* and *Tan-Andal* Rebalancing: Protecting Living Families, Not Dead Shells

During the two decades following *Molina*, the constitutional mandate to protect marriage as an inviolable social institution was frequently invoked to justify an ultra-strict, nearly insurmountable evidentiary threshold that denied almost every Article 36 petition reaching the Supreme Court.[^89] [^90]  Beginning with [*Ngo Te v. Yu-Te*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/48851) (2009) and [*Kalaw v. Fernandez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/59147) (2015) and culminating in [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), [*Quilpan v. Quilpan*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67927) (2021), and [*Soto v. Reyes-Soto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70372) (2026), the Supreme Court fundamentally recalibrated the constitutional inquiry.[^91] [^92] [^93] [^94] [^95] 

The Court explained that reading Article XV, Section 2 (protecting marriage) in isolation from Article XV, Section 1 (strengthening family solidarity and promoting its total development) distorts the Constitution's design:[^const-1987] [^96] 

* **Marriage as a means to the family:** The Constitution protects marriage *because* it is the foundation of the family; when a union is void from the beginning due to a spouse's psychological incapacity, there is no true marital union to preserve, and forcing the parties and their children to remain bound to a destructive, abusive, or hollow legal shell subverts rather than promotes family solidarity.[^97] [^98] [^99] 
* **Compassion and human dignity:** In extinguishing a marriage that exists only on paper and has long caused psychological trauma to the spouses and their offspring, the judiciary does not weaken the institution of marriage; rather, it upholds the dignity of the human person and honors the sacredness of genuine marriage by refusing to equate it with a union devoid of mutual love, respect, fidelity, and support.[^100] [^101] [^102] [^103] 
* **Constitutional protection of autonomous families:** As [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) emphasized, Article XV of the Constitution protects healthy, functioning families regardless of whether the parents are formally married, and the State has no constitutional interest in perpetuating the agony of a marriage that was void *ab initio* under Article 36.[^const-1987] [^104]

---

## 3. Doctrinal Evolution Across Three Jurisprudential Eras

The decisions below trace the development from the earlier guidelines to the current legal concept of psychological incapacity.

### 3.1 The Early Formative Period: *Salita*, *Santos*, and *Chi Ming Tsoi* (1994–1997)

The early procedural case concerned a bill of particulars already filed. In [*Salita v. Magtolis*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1994/jun1994/gr_106429_1994.html), the trial court granted a request for a bill of particulars and the husband supplied one.[^105] The Supreme Court held the amplification sufficient and refused to require further evidentiary detail.[^106]

Seven months later, on January 4, 1995, the Supreme Court sitting *En Banc* promulgated its first full merits decision on Article 36 in [*Santos v. Court of Appeals*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) (1995), penned by Justice Jose C. Vitug.[^107]  In [*Santos*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html), the husband, Leouel Santos, sought to nullify his marriage to Julia Bedia, who had left for the United States to work as a nurse and failed to return home; the decision also records a telephone call she made on January 1, 1989.[^108]  Examining the Family Code deliberations and Canon 1095 commentaries (notably Velarde and the Rota jurisprudence), the Court ruled that Julia's refusal to return was at most willful abandonment (a ground for legal separation under Article 55), not psychological incapacity.[^109] [^110]  Crucially, [*Santos*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) formulated the enduring tripartite characterization of psychological incapacity:

1. **Gravity (*Gravedad*):** The condition must be grave or serious enough to bring about the disability of the party to assume the essential obligations of marriage, not mere mild characterological peculiarities, mood changes, occasional emotional outbursts, or refusal, neglect or difficulty, much less ill will.[^111] 
2. **Juridical Antecedence (*Antecedencia*):** The incapacity must be rooted in the history of the party antedating the marriage, although the overt manifestations may emerge only after the wedding.[^112] 
3. **Incurability (*Incurabilidad*):** The incapacity must be incurable, or even if it were otherwise, the cure would be beyond the means of the party involved.[^113] 

In January 1997, shortly before *Molina*, the Court decided [*Chi Ming Tsoi v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/34416) (1997), affirming the nullity of a marriage where the husband, despite possessing no physical or physiological erectile defect, senselessly and protractedly refused to consummate the marriage through sexual intercourse throughout ten months of cohabitation.[^114]  The Court held that procreation and the mutual physical expression of love are essential marital obligations rooted in the universal human drive, and that an abnormal, unexplained refusal to engage in sexual intimacy over almost a year of cohabitation, coupled with complete insensitivity and lack of remorse toward the spouse's suffering, manifested a grave psychological incapacity existing at the time of marriage.[^115] 

### 3.2 The *Molina* Straitjacket (1997–2021)

Alarmed by reports of trial courts granting Article 36 petitions with perceived laxity (so-called annulment mills treating simple marital incompatibility as psychological incapacity), the Supreme Court *En Banc* promulgated [*Republic v. Court of Appeals and Molina*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/34463) on February 13, 1997, penned by Justice Artemio V. Panganiban.[^116]  In *Molina*, the Court reversed the lower courts' declaration of nullity where the husband (Reynaldo Molina) exhibited immaturity, irresponsibility, financial dependence on his parents, dishonesty regarding finances, and eventual abandonment of the family.[^117]  Holding that these traits showed only difficulty, refusal, or neglect rather than an incapacity of a psychological nature, the Court laid down **eight mandatory guidelines** to govern the disposition of all Article 36 petitions:[^118] 

1. **Burden of proof:** The burden of proof to show the nullity of the marriage belongs to the plaintiff, and any doubt should be resolved in favor of the existence and continuation of the marriage and against its dissolution and nullity.[^119] 
2. **Root cause medically or clinically identified:** The root cause of the psychological incapacity must be (a) medically or clinically identified, (b) alleged in the complaint, (c) sufficiently proven by experts, and (d) clearly explained in the decision.[^120]  Article 36 requires that the incapacity must be psychological, not physical, although its manifestations and/or symptoms may be physical.[^121] 
3. **Existence at the time of marriage:** The incapacity must be proven to be existing at the time of the celebration of the marriage, even if its manifestation is perceived only after solemnization.[^122] 
4. **Permanent or incurable:** Such incapacity must also be shown to be medically or clinically permanent or incurable, whether absolute or relative only in regard to the other spouse, not necessarily to everyone of the same sex.[^123] 
5. **Gravity:** Such illness must be grave enough to bring about the disability of the party to assume the essential obligations of marriage.[^124] 
6. **Essential marital obligations:** The essential marital obligations must be those embraced by Articles 68 up to 71 of the Family Code as regards the husband and wife as well as Articles 220, 221, and 225 of the same Code in regard to parents and their children, and non-complied-with obligations must be stated in the petition, proven by evidence, and included in the text of the decision.[^125] [^126] [^127] [^128] [^129] [^130] [^131] [^132] 
7. **Respect for Catholic Matrimonial Tribunal interpretations:** Interpretations given by the National Appellate Matrimonial Tribunal of the Catholic Church in the Philippines, while not controlling or decisive, should be given great respect by our courts.[^133] 
8. **OSG and Prosecutor certification:** The trial court must order the prosecuting attorney or fiscal and the Solicitor General to appear as counsel for the state, and no decision shall be handed down unless the Solicitor General issues a certification, which will be quoted in the decision, briefly stating therein his reasons for his agreement or opposition to the petition.[^134] 

Although [*Marcos v. Marcos*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/38116) (2000) clarified three years later that a *personal* medical or psychiatric examination of the respondent spouse is not a *conditio sine qua non* if the totality of evidence suffices, and A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC (effective March 15, 2003) formally dispensed with Guideline No. 8's OSG certification requirement and stated that expert opinion need not be alleged in the petition, in actual appellate practice Guideline No. 2 and Guideline No. 4 operated as an ironclad clinical filter.[^135] [^136] [^137]  Across scores of decisions (including [*Hernandez v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/37001) (1999), [*Republic v. Dagdag*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/50120) (2001), [*Pesca v. Pesca*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/52118) (2001), [*Choa v. Choa*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/51704) (2002), [*Dedel v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/46669) (2004), [*Republic v. Quintero-Hamano*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/46281) (2004), [*Carating-Siayngco v. Siayngco*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/46167) (2004), [*Republic v. Iyoy*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/43815) (2005), [*Republic v. Cuison-Melgar*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/40740) (2006), [*Perez-Ferraris v. Ferraris*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/41701) (2006), [*Tongol v. Tongol*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/44668) (2007), [*Paras v. Paras*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/44139) (2007), [*Navales v. Navales*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/45754) (2008), [*Ting v. Velez-Ting*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/49022) (2009), [*Suazo v. Suazo*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/53719) (2010), [*Toring v. Toring*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/54167) (2010), [*Agraviador v. Amparo-Agraviador*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/34985) (2010), [*Ochosa v. Alano*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/35099) (2011), [*Republic v. De Gracia*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/56531) (2014), [*Matudan v. Republic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/62670) (2016), [*Castillo v. Republic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/62823) (2017), [*Del Rosario v. Del Rosario*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/62876) (2017), [*Singson v. Singson*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/63850) (2018), [*Espina-Dan v. Dan*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/64126) (2018), and [*Cortez v. Cortez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/65103) (2019)), petitions were routinely dismissed because the clinical psychologist had not personally interviewed the respondent spouse, had failed to trace the disorder to early childhood through independent informants, or had diagnosed a personality disorder that the Court deemed insufficiently medically rooted.[^138] [^139] [^140] [^141] [^142] [^143] [^144] [^145] [^146] [^147] [^148] [^149] [^150] [^151] [^152] [^153] [^154] [^155] [^156] [^157] [^158] [^159] [^160] [^161] [^162] 

### 3.3 Cracks in the Straitjacket: *Antonio*, *Ngo Te*, and *Kalaw* (2006–2015)

Even before 2021, several decisions attempted to loosen the clinical vise of *Molina*:

* ***Antonio v. Reyes* (2006):** The alleged deceptions must be stated as recorded. The husband alleged that the wife misrepresented herself as a psychiatrist and claimed a psychology degree, invented recording-industry connections and altered her payslip.[^163] The Supreme Court reinstated the judgment declaring the marriage null and void.[^164]
* ***Ngo Te v. Yu-Te* (2009):** Penned by Justice Antonio Eduardo B. Nachura, the Court explicitly lamented that *Molina* had functioned as a strait-jacket that had unintentionally defeated the resilience intended by the Family Code Revision Committee.[^165]  Observing that under *Molina* almost all that were filed under Article 36 were denied, [*Ngo Te*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/48851) urged courts to abandon rigid checklists and judge each petition on a case-to-case basis, declaring void a whirlwind marriage contracted under manipulative threats by a wife afflicted with Antisocial Personality Disorder and a husband with Dependent Personality Disorder.[^166] 
* **The Reaction in [*Ting v. Velez-Ting*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/49022) (2009) and [*Suazo v. Suazo*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/53719) (2010):** Only six weeks after [*Ngo Te*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/48851), a different Division in [*Ting v. Velez-Ting*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/49022) (2009) pushed back, explaining that [*Ngo Te*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/48851) did not abandon *Molina* (which only the Court *En Banc* could overturn under Article VIII, Section 4(3) of the Constitution) and claiming that *Molina* merely prevented abusive, fabricated petitions.[^167] [^168] 
* ***Azcueta v. Republic* (2009), *Halili v. Santos-Halili*, and *Camacho-Reyes v. Reyes* (2010):** The Court granted nullity where Dependent, Antisocial, or Narcissistic Personality Disorders were corroborated by long-standing behavioral patterns and independent witnesses.[^169] [^170] 
* ***Kalaw v. Fernandez* (2015):** After initially denying the petition in [*Kalaw v. Fernandez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/59147) (2011) on a strict reading of *Molina*, the Special First Division on reconsideration in [*Kalaw v. Fernandez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/59147) (2015), penned by Justice Lucas P. Bersamin, reversed itself and declared the marriage void.[^171] [^172]  The Court echoed [*Ngo Te*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/48851)'s critique of the *Molina* straitjacket, held that the frequency of a wife's mahjong gambling and habitual abandonment of her young children unattended demonstrated a grave, deeply rooted inability to fulfill parental and conjugal duties, and accorded weight to expert psychiatric and canon law evaluations supported by corroborative testimony.[^173] [^174] [^175] [^176] [^177] 

### 3.4 The *Tan-Andal v. Andal* Paradigm Shift (2021–Present)

On May 11, 2021, the Supreme Court *En Banc* promulgated [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (G.R. No. 196359), penned by Associate Justice Marvic M.V.F. Leonen, resolving the doctrinal tension that had persisted between *Molina* and [*Ngo Te*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/48851) for over a decade.[^178]  Confronting the case of Mario Andal, whose severe, lifelong drug addiction, financial irresponsibility, paranoia, and reckless endangerment of his daughter (including exposing the child to his drug-smoking room and driving erratically) had destroyed his marriage to Rosanna Tan-Andal, the Court *En Banc* conducted a comprehensive re-examination of Article 36 and formally modified the *Molina* guidelines:[^179] 

1. **Quantum of Proof Fixed at Clear and Convincing Evidence:** To harmonize the presumption of validity of marriage with the civil nature of nullity proceedings, the plaintiff must prove psychological incapacity by **clear and convincing evidence**, a quantum higher than mere preponderance of evidence required in ordinary civil cases, but lower than proof beyond reasonable doubt required in criminal prosecutions.[^180] 
2. **Abandonment of Medical/Clinical Root-Cause Requirement (*Molina* Guideline No. 2 Abandoned):** Psychological incapacity is **not a medical or mental illness**, nor is it a personality disorder as defined in the *Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders* (DSM).[^181]  Rather, it must be understood as a **durable or enduring aspect of a person's personality** (called their **personality structure**) that manifests itself through clear acts of dysfunctionality that undermine the family, such that the spouse's personality structure makes it impossible for them to understand and, more important, to comply with their essential marital obligations.[^182] 
3. **Dispensing with Mandatory Expert Psychiatric or Psychological Testimony:** Because psychological incapacity is a legal concept rather than a clinical illness, **there is no need for an expert opinion from a psychologist or psychiatrist** to prove it.[^183]  **Ordinary witnesses** who have been present in the life of the spouses before the marriage may testify on behaviors that they have consistently observed from the supposedly incapacitated spouse; from these lay observations, the judge may discern whether a persistent pattern of dysfunctional behavior existed prior to and during the marriage.[^184] 
4. **Admissibility of Expert Opinion Without Personal Examination:** When a party nevertheless chooses to present a psychiatrist or clinical psychologist, **a personal medical or psychological examination of the respondent spouse is not required** for a declaration of psychological incapacity.[^185]  Psychiatric and psychological experts routinely form valid clinical and forensic assessments from collateral histories, documentary records, and interviews with the petitioning spouse, relatives, and acquaintances.[^186] 
5. **Reconceptualization of Incurability as Legal and Relational (*Molina* Guideline No. 4 Modified):** Because psychological incapacity is not an illness, the third [*Santos*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) requisite, **incurability**, is **not taken in the medical or clinical sense**, but in the **legal sense**.[^187]  This means that the incapacity is so **enduring and persistent with respect to a specific partner**, and contemplates a situation where the couple's respective **personality structures are so incompatible and antagonistic** that the union results in the inevitable and irreparable breakdown of the marriage.[^188] 
6. **Preservation of Gravity ([*Santos*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) First Requisite Retained):** The requisite of **gravity** is retained: the incapacity must be caused by a genuinely serious psychic cause, explicitly excluding mild characterological peculiarities, mood changes, occasional emotional outbursts, as well as mere difficulty, refusal, or neglect in the performance of marital obligations.[^189] 
7. **Preservation of Juridical Antecedence ([*Santos*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) Second Requisite Retained):** The requisite of **juridical antecedence** is mandated by the statutory text (at the time of the celebration) and is retained: the incapacity must be proven to have existed at the time of the wedding, even if it manifests only afterward, and may be proven through detailed testimony regarding the spouse's prior lived experiences, family background, childhood, adolescent habits, or pre-marital courtship behavior.[^190] 
8. **Essential Marital Obligations Including Duties to Children (*Molina* Guideline No. 6 Clarified):** The essential marital obligations remain those under Articles 68 to 71 of the Family Code between spouses, as well as Articles 220, 221, and 225 toward their children; however, the Court clarified that not every obligation toward children is automatically an essential marital obligation, though a spouse's gross, enduring failure to care for and protect the couple's children when indicative of an inability to form a family consortium directly violates the core marital covenant.[^191] [^192] [^193] [^194] [^195] [^196] [^197] [^198] 
9. **Persuasive Value of Canonical Decrees (*Molina* Guideline No. 7 Retained):** Canonical interpretations and decrees of the National Appellate Matrimonial Tribunal of the Catholic Church remain non-binding but persuasive.[^199] 

Post-[*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) decisions reached different results on their own records. The chronological survey below records the final dispositions separately from the granting or denial of an appeal.

| Doctrinal Dimension | *Santos v. CA* (1995) | *Republic v. CA & Molina* (1997) | [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021–Present) |
|:--- |:--- |:--- |:--- |
| **Nature of Psychological Incapacity** | Grave psychic inability to assume essential marital obligations; derived from Canon 1095(3) | Medical or clinical mental illness or personality disorder requiring psychiatric identification | **Purely legal concept**; an enduring **personality structure** and interpersonal incompatibility |
| **Quantum of Proof** | General civil burden (presumption of validity of marriage) | Any doubt resolved in favor of marriage continuation; strict scrutiny | **Clear and convincing evidence** (between preponderance and proof beyond reasonable doubt) |
| **Root Cause Identification** | Must be rooted in the party's history antedating the marriage | Must be **medically or clinically identified**, alleged in complaint, and proven by experts | **No medical/clinical root cause required**; proven through ordinary witnesses on pre-marital lived experiences |
| **Expert Psychological Testimony** | Not explicitly mandated, though helpful | **Mandatory** (sufficiently proven by experts) under Guideline No. 2 | **Dispensed with**; ordinary witnesses suffice, though expert testimony remains admissible and helpful |
| **Personal Examination of Respondent** | Not addressed | Treated by many Divisions as practically necessary for credibility | **Expressly not required**; experts may rely on collateral interviews and records |
| **Meaning of Incurability** | Incurable, or cure beyond the party's means | **Medically or clinically** permanent or incurable | **Legal and relational sense**: enduring, persistent incompatibility with the specific spouse leading to inevitable breakdown |
| **OSG Certification** | Not required | **Mandatory** under Guideline No. 8 | **Dispensed with** (repealed by A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC; OSG participates as counsel for the State) |

---

## 4. The Concept of "Personality Structure" Versus Mental or Clinical Illness

### 4.1 Demedicalization of Article 36

The central intellectual breakthrough of [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021) lies in its explicit repudiation of the medicalization of psychological incapacity.[^200]  Tracing the records of the Family Code Revision Committee, the Court demonstrated that the codifiers initially considered using the adjective mentally incapacitated, but consciously rejected it in favor of psychologically incapacitated precisely to distinguish the concept from clinical psychiatric disorders or physical defects.[^201]  When *Molina* in 1997 equated psychological incapacity with a mental illness whose root cause had to be medically or clinically identified by psychiatrists and proved to be medically or clinically permanent or incurable, it grafted a clinical pathology requirement onto Article 36 that the framers had specifically rejected.[^202] [^203] 

Moreover, [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) highlighted the social and forensic evils produced by the clinical approach:[^204] 

1. **Pathologizing and stigmatizing litigants:** Forcing a petitioner to brand their spouse (and the parent of their children) with a clinical *DSM* mental disorder or psychiatric illness inflicted unnecessary social stigma and hostility in already fractured families;[^205] 
2. **Encouraging cookie-cutter psychiatric reports:** Because *Molina* demanded a clinical label, litigation devolved into a battle of paid forensic psychologists who mechanically slotted marital histories into *DSM* Axis II labels (such as Narcissistic, Antisocial, Histrionic, Dependent, or Borderline Personality Disorder), which appellate courts then dismissed as conclusory or one-sided whenever the respondent refused to be interviewed;[^206] [^207] [^208]  and
3. **Restricting access to justice:** Requiring expensive psychiatric testing and expert witness fees erected a prohibitive financial barrier against indigent and middle-class Filipinos seeking relief from void marriages.[^209] 

### 4.2 Defining "Personality Structure" and Relational Anomaly

Under [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) and its progeny (including [*Estella v. Perez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67987) (2021), [*Alberto v. Alberto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68353) (2022), [*Quiogue, Jr. v. Quiogue*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68704) (2022), [*Georfo v. Republic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68945) (2023), [*Boado v. Galvez-Boado*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69865) (2024), [*Green v. Green*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69822) (2025), [*Kusk v. Kusk*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70120) (2025), and [*Soto v. Reyes-Soto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70372) (2026)), psychological incapacity consists of a **durable or enduring aspect of a person's personality**, termed their **personality structure,** that manifests itself through **clear acts of dysfunctionality that undermine the family**, such that the spouse's personality structure makes it impossible for them to understand and, more importantly, to comply with their essential marital obligations.[^210] [^211] [^212] [^213] [^214] [^215] [^216] [^217] [^218] 

This personality-structure framework carries three critical corollaries:

* **Functional over diagnostic:** A person may be a high-functioning, successful professional, entrepreneur, or civic figure in society at large (possessing normal intelligence, business acumen, and social charm) yet harbor a deeply ingrained personality structure that renders them completely blind and unresponsive to the interpersonal obligations of marriage and family life, as recognized in [*Antonio v. Reyes*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/40916) (2006), [*Kalaw v. Fernandez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/59147) (2015), [*Montealto-Laylo v. Ymbang*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67993) (2021), [*Green v. Green*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69822) (2025), and [*Soto v. Reyes-Soto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70372) (2026).[^219] [^220] [^221] [^222] [^223] 
* **The spouse's account of their conduct:** The evidence may include the spouse's own explanation of the marital conflict. In [*Quiogue, Jr. v. Quiogue*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68704), the Supreme Court stated:

Apart from the chronicity of Antonio's infractions, the Court also notes that there is no clear recognition on his part that fidelity is one of his essential obligations to his wife Maribel.

In the Psychiatric Evaluation, Dr. Garcia quoted Antonio saying: "As a husband, I'm practically a good husband; but I would always be cheating on my wife." From his perspective, his illicit affairs are minor incidents which Maribel should have overlooked or dealt with differently. It also did not escape the Court's attention that Antonio has a distorted concept of a wife. During his interviews with Dr. Garcia, Antonio referred to Maribel as a "good housewife," "because she is not employed anyway." He described Maribel as a dedicated mother "because she has no work, and she can always be with kids." In return, Antonio "compensated her" by giving her a monthly allowance for household expenses. It is evident that Antonio considers himself superior to his wife. He does not consider Maribel as a partner, hence, there is a constant need for him to look for affection outside the marriage.

Moreover, Antonio blamed Maribel's nagging and tactlessness for the demise of their marriage, saying that her actions drove him away. He admitted that he was weak in not being able to control his womanizing, but Maribel is at fault for not doing anything to win him back. He did not perceive his wife's actions as a sign of despair or her own peculiar way of fighting for their marriage. Antonio focused on Maribel's anger and its effects on him. Antonio's failure to show sincere remorse for his blatant infidelity and the lack of desire to fix his ways to save their marriage clearly amount to psychological incapacity, which is grave in nature.[^224]
* **The specific marital relationship:** The inquiry includes the particular spouses. In [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507), the Supreme Court stated:

Reading together the deliberations of the Code Committee and our rulings in Santos and Molina, we hold that the psychological incapacity contemplated in Article 36 of the Family Code is incurable, not in the medical, but in the legal sense; hence, the third Molina guideline is amended accordingly. This means that the incapacity is so enduring and persistent with respect to a specific partner, and contemplates a situation where the couple's respective personality structures are so incompatible and antagonistic that the only result of the union would be the inevitable and irreparable breakdown of the marriage. "An undeniable pattern of such persisting failure to be a present, loving, faithful, respectful, and supportive spouse must be established so as to demonstrate that there is indeed a psychological anomaly or incongruity in the spouse relative to the other."

With respect to gravity, the requirement is retained, not in the sense that the psychological incapacity must be shown to be a serious or dangerous illness, but that "mild characterological peculiarities, mood changes, occasional emotional outbursts" are excluded. The psychological incapacity cannot be mere "refusal, neglect, or difficulty, much less ill will." In other words, it must be shown that the incapacity is caused by a genuinely serious psychic cause.[^225]

---

## 5. The Three Essential Requisites of Psychological Incapacity

While [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) discarded *Molina*'s clinical pathology requirements, it reaffirmed and refined the three foundational requisites first articulated in [*Santos v. Court of Appeals*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1995/jan1995/gr_112019_1995.html) (1995): **gravity**, **juridical antecedence**, and **incurability**.[^226] [^227] [^228]  Every successful petition under Article 36 must establish all three requisites by clear and convincing evidence.[^229] [^230] [^231] 

### 5.1 Gravity (*Gravedad*): True Incapacity Versus Mere Refusal, Neglect, or Difficulty

**Gravity** requires that the psychological condition or personality structure be serious or grave enough to bring about the **disability or inability** (*incapacidad*) of the party to assume the essential obligations of marriage.[^232] [^233] [^234]  In every era of Article 36 jurisprudence, the Supreme Court has drawn a sharp doctrinal boundary between *inability* (*can not*) and *willful refusal, neglect, or difficulty* (*will not*):

* **Exclusion of mild characterological peculiarities:** Gravity excludes mild characterological peculiarities, mood changes, occasional emotional outbursts, temperamental friction, or ordinary marital bickering.[^235] [^236] [^237] [^238] 
* **Exclusion of mere difficulty, refusal, or ill will:** When a spouse is fully capable of understanding and performing marital duties (and indeed performs them during the early years of marriage) but later chooses to abandon the family, engage in extramarital affairs, squander money, or mistreat the other spouse out of boredom, selfishness, resentment, or ill will, the conduct constitutes a willful breach of contract or a ground for legal separation under Article 55, not psychological incapacity under Article 36.[^239] [^240] [^241] [^242] [^243] [^244] [^245] [^246] 
* **Frequency is not a substitute for the legal requisites:** The Supreme Court stated in [*Quiogue, Jr. v. Quiogue*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68704):

Of course, this is not to say that the Court will intuitively declare a marriage void for a single act of marital indiscretion. Infidelity is not measured in terms of frequency. To be considered as a form of psychological incapacity, infidelity must satisfy the requirements of

(1) gravity or severity,

(2) antecedence, and

(3) legal incurability or persistence during the marriage.

(1) Juridical antecedence:

In this case, Antonio's Chronic infidelity is not only comprised of multiple illicit amorous relations. As detailed in the Psychiatric Evaluation by Dr. Garcia, his affairs are not casual mistakes as these were shown to be deeply rooted in his psychopathology which was in place even before his marriage.[^247]

### 5.2 Juridical Antecedence (*Antecedencia*): Existence at the Time of Celebration

**Juridical antecedence** flows directly from the statutory text of Article 36: the party must have been psychologically incapacitated **at the time of the celebration** of the marriage, even if the incapacity becomes manifest only after its solemnization.[^248] [^249] [^250]  Because Article 36 renders a marriage void *ab initio* rather than dissolving a valid marriage by divorce, a psychological breakdown triggered solely by post-marriage events (such as financial setbacks, overseas separation, midlife infidelity, or in-law conflicts) without a pre-existing root in the spouse's personality structure prior to the wedding cannot ground a declaration of nullity.[^251] [^252] [^253] [^254] [^255] [^256] 

Juridical antecedence must be assessed with the evidence of the relationship. In [*Republic v. Ramoran-Wong*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70390), the Supreme Court stated:

Juridical antecedence is established by showing that the psychological incapacity exists at the time of the celebration, even if it only manifests during the marriage. It may be proven by "testimonies describing the environment where the supposedly incapacitated spouse lived that may have led to a particular behavior."

While it is difficult to pinpoint when the psychological incapacity existed, it is enough that the petitioner show that the incapacity, "in all reasonable likelihood," already exists at the time the marriage was celebrated. Since the spouses will only assume marital obligations after the marriage, their psychological capacity to fulfill those obligations will only manifest after the celebration of the marriage.

Incurability must be viewed in the legal, not medical, sense. Veering away from the medical orientation, the third Molina guideline was amended. Psychological incapacity is not a medical illness which can be cured: it must be "so enduring and persistent with respect to a specific partner, and contemplates a situation where the couple's respective personality structures are so incompatible and antagonistic that the only result of the union would be the inevitable and irreparable breakdown of the marriage."

To satisfy the requirement of incurability, there must be a showing of an "undeniable pattern of such persisting failure to be a present, loving, faithful, respectful, and supportive spouse that must be established so as to demonstrate that there is indeed a psychological anomaly or incongruity in the spouse relative to the other."

The requirement on the gravity of the psychological incapacity was retained, which must be "caused by a genuinely psychic cause." It must not be mere "mild characterological peculiarities, mood changes, occasional emotional outbursts," nor "mere refusal, neglect, difficulty, much less ill will." (Citations omitted)[^257]

1. **Childhood and family-of-origin environment:** Evidence of severe childhood trauma, parental abandonment, abusive or authoritarian upbringing, exposure to domestic violence or parental infidelity, extreme spoilt or overprotective parenting (*coddling*), or dysfunctional family norms that shaped the spouse's warped concept of family and interpersonal commitment, as established in [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021) (enabling family dynamics and adolescent drug use), [*Estella v. Perez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67987) (2021), [*De Silva v. De Silva*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67852) (2021), [*Alberto v. Alberto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68353) (2022), [*Laroco v. Laroco*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69077) (2022), [*Georfo v. Republic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68945) (2023), [*Boado v. Galvez-Boado*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69865) (2024), [*Green v. Green*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69822) (2025), [*Kusk v. Kusk*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70120) (2025), [*Republic v. Ramoran-Wong*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70390) (2026) (early parental separation and irresponsible and unfaithful father witnessed by the respondent's father and cousin), and [*Soto v. Reyes-Soto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70372) (2026) (strict, disciplinarian, and emotionally detached upbringing);[^258] [^259] [^260] [^261] [^262] [^263] [^264] [^265] [^266] [^267] [^268] 
2. **The connection between earlier experiences and marital conduct:** The earlier history must be considered with the actual relationship. In [*Quiogue, Jr. v. Quiogue*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68704), the Supreme Court explained:

In Tan-Andal v. Andal, the Court recognized that a person's behavior is determined not only by certain genetic predispositions but is also influenced by his or her environment. One that is particularly significant is the character of the parents as witnessed by a child during the formative years. Here, Dr. Garcia's psychiatric evaluation invariably shows that Antonio's proclivity to act on his sexual impulses is deeply rooted in a psychological condition that existed before the celebration of their marriage.

Contrary to the CA's observation, Dr. Garcia was able to demonstrate how Antonio's childhood and adolescent years – which were spent dealing with his philandering father and impervious mother, heavily affected the way he perceives romantic relationships. Antonio's father sired eleven children, with four other women, and was never discreet about his illicit affairs. Women would often look for his father and ask for financial support. As a child, Antonio thought this was the only role of his father. Dr. Garcia also noted that Antonio lacked the proper attention from his mother. This led to his attention-seeking demeanor and dependent personality trait which fuel his propensity to look for thrill in his relationships. For instance, Antonio recalled that after school, he would spend the rest of the day with a tutor. When he comes home at night, his mother would not be there as she would always play mahjong elsewhere. Growing up, he detested his father, but his mother would force him to show respect because they are being well provided for. Unfortunately, Antonio mirrored his father's ways. As a young adult, he engaged in several short­-lived, overlapping relationships, including the one with Maribel. They dated when he still had another girlfriend. After two years, Maribel got pregnant, so he was forced to marry her. However, the marriage did not prove to be a catalyst for Antonio as he repeatedly committed marital indiscretions. Soon enough, the spouses fell into a pernicious cycle of discovery, reprisal, forgiveness, and then a new illicit affair. Clearly, Antonio's inability to maintain a monogamous relationship with his wife can be traced to his dysfunctional childhood.[^269]
3. **A successful application of the standard:** A case must be classified by the Supreme Court's own conclusion. In [*Javate-Asejo v. Asejo*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68921), the Supreme Court stated:

Contrary to the finding of the RTC that the psychological incapacity of Justiniano has been established through a preponderance of evidence, this Court finds that the evidence presented is more compelling, and ought to be properly regarded as clear and convincing proof.

The expert testimony was anchored on interviews with unbiased witnesses and has been corroborated by the other witnesses presented by Constancia. Justiniano's persisting psychological condition was identified, its history traced from his childhood and upbringing, and its manifestations prior to and throughout their marriage have been demonstrated by evidence beyond reproach. Thus, Constancia was able to discharge the requisite burden of proof.

Moreover, based on the foregoing discussions, this Court has noted that even the evidence presented by the State failed to dissuade and have actually buttressed the case for declaring the nullity of this marriage.

All told, the records speak clearly and convincingly that Justiniano is suffering from psychological incapacity, of such gravity, antecedence, and incurability, that prevents him from recognizing his essential marital obligations and renders his marriage to Constancia null and void ab initio.

WHEREFORE, the Petition for Review on Certiorari is GRANTED. The Decision, dated November 29, 2018, and the Resolution, dated May 8, 2019, of the Court of Appeals in CA-G.R. CV No. 110708 are REVERSED. The Decision, dated June 19, 2017, of the Regional Trial Court, Branch 21, Santiago City, is REINSTATED.[^270]

### 5.3 Incurability (*Incurabilidad*): Legal, Enduring, and Relational Incompatibility

Prior to 2021, *Molina* Guideline No. 4 required proof that the psychological incapacity was medically or clinically permanent or incurable.[^271]  In [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), the Supreme Court recognized the scientific and logical anomaly of that requirement: because psychological incapacity is not a medical illness, demanding proof of medical incurability asked courts to evaluate a non-existent medical condition.[^272] 

Accordingly, [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) redefined **incurability in the legal sense**:[^273] 

* **Enduring and persistent vis-à-vis the specific partner:** Legal incurability means that the incapacity is **so enduring and persistent with respect to a specific partner** that the couple's respective personality structures are **so incompatible and antagonistic** that the union results in the **inevitable and irreparable breakdown of the marriage**.[^274] [^275] 
* **Relative to the specific partner:** The governing formulation appears in [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507):

Reading together the deliberations of the Code Committee and our rulings in Santos and Molina, we hold that the psychological incapacity contemplated in Article 36 of the Family Code is incurable, not in the medical, but in the legal sense; hence, the third Molina guideline is amended accordingly. This means that the incapacity is so enduring and persistent with respect to a specific partner, and contemplates a situation where the couple's respective personality structures are so incompatible and antagonistic that the only result of the union would be the inevitable and irreparable breakdown of the marriage. "An undeniable pattern of such persisting failure to be a present, loving, faithful, respectful, and supportive spouse must be established so as to demonstrate that there is indeed a psychological anomaly or incongruity in the spouse relative to the other."[^276]
* **Proof through repeated failed attempts and prolonged separation:** Legal incurability is demonstrated empirically when repeated efforts at counseling, family intervention, forgiveness, cohabitation adjustments, and years of separation fail to alter the afflicted spouse's dysfunctional behavior or elicit genuine insight into their marital obligations.[^277] [^278] [^279] [^280]

---

## 6. Essential Marital Obligations Under the Family Code

Article 36 does not penalize personality eccentricities in the abstract; the incapacity must specifically disable a spouse from complying with **the essential marital obligations of marriage.**[^281] [^282] [^283]  Both *Molina* (Guideline No. 6) and [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) locate these essential obligations in two clusters of Family Code provisions: first, the inter-spousal obligations under **Articles 68 to 71**; and second, the parental obligations toward the children of the marriage under **Articles 220, 221, and 225**.[^284] [^285] [^286] [^287] [^288] [^289] [^290] [^291] [^292] [^293] 

### 6.1 Inter-Spousal Obligations (Articles 68 to 71)

Under Title III (Rights and Obligations Between Husband and Wife) of the Family Code, the core covenantal duties of the spouses comprise: 

1. **Article 68 (Cohabitation, Mutual Love, Respect, Fidelity, Help, and Support):** The husband and wife are obliged to live together, observe mutual love, respect, and fidelity, and render mutual help and support.[^294] [^295] [^296] [^297] 
   * *Cohabitation and emotional obligations:* The circumstances of distance between the spouses must be considered on their own record. In [*Boado v. Galvez-Boado*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69865), the Supreme Court stated:

Based on the evidence presented by petitioner, he clearly and convincingly proved his psychological incapacity, specifically its juridical antecedence. It remains undisputed that petitioner grew up with a strict mother who "offered him too much demand and too little warmth." As a result, he maintained emotional distance in relationships, learning how to bottle up his true emotions and feelings just to keep the peace. This is why petitioner can perform essential marital obligations tied to providing the family's material needs, such as financial support, but lacked in providing emotional needs, the most basic of which is physical presence and companionship.

Indeed, there is evidence of the emotional distance between the spouses, which was exacerbated by the frequent physical distance between them due to their jobs. A review of the facts shows that from 1999-2002, when the parties were still dating, they were only physically together for about a year, i.e., in the years 1999-2000. From 2000 to 2002, they were apart. While they communicated over the phone, they often did so just to fight.

From the time they were married in 2002 until they separated for good in 2016, petitioner and private respondent were only physically together for about five years:

(1) from 2005-2008, when petitioner went home from Saudi Arabia;

(2) from 2011-2012, when the parties reconciled; and

(3) from 2025-2016, when petitioner went home from the United States. These show that petitioner can maintain a relationship only from a distance, frequently keeping his spouse at arm's length.

Besides, just because a spouse was once able to perform some marital obligations does not mean that they cannot be subsequently incapable of fulfilling some of the other obligations. This is why Article 36 of the Family Code states that psychological incapacity can manifest after the marriage's solemnization. In the case of petitioner, his psychological incapacity fully manifested later in the marriage, specifically in 2016, 14 years into the marriage.[^298]
   * *Mutual love and respect:* A case need not involve violence or infidelity to fall under Article 36. In [*Soto v. Reyes-Soto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70372), the Supreme Court stated:

To be sure, the psychological incapacity that undermined this unfortunate union and prevented it from maturing into a genuine and fulfilling marriage is not of the kind that is immediately apparent. There was no violence, no infidelity, and none of the overtly traumatic events often associated with marriages burdened by psychological incapacity. For this reason, the Court has consistently stressed that every Article 36 petition must be examined with particular care. Each determination must rest on the totality of the evidence and not on the presence or absence of dramatic markers of marital failure.

Every relationship bears its own shape. Some marriages are void from the very beginning, not because of loud or conspicuous dysfunction but because of quiet and deeply rooted tears, anxieties, and limitations that prevent the spouses from understanding and assuming the essential obligations of marriage. The unspoken inability to fully connect with one's partner can be as destructive as conflict that is overt and volatile. Silent dysfunction, manifested in emotional distance, withdrawal, and resignation, can be no less corrosive to the marital bond than more visible forms of incapacity, and it is no less within the contemplation of Article 36 of the Family Code.

Thus, the Court concludes that this case falls squarely within the precise compass of Article 36 of the Family Code, as understood and refined in jurisprudence. Article 36 recognizes that some unions are void from the beginning not because of moral fault, but because the spouses were never psychologically capable of entering into the kind of partnership that marriage demands. As the Court emphasized in Tan-Andal, the law protects the institution of marriage not by insisting on its preservation at all costs but by distinguishing true marriages from those that, from their inception, lacked the essential psychological foundations for mutual commitment, intimacy, and shared life.

Properly understood, Article 36 of the Family Code is not hostile to marriage. It is, in truth, protective of this institution, it acknowledges that marriage, is among, the most meaningful acts two people can undertake together. To compel individuals to remain bound in a union that, from the start, functioned as a psychological case rather than a shared life of freedom and meaning does not strengthen marriage. It only transforms marriage into a site of quiet misery, erodes its dignity, and risks deterring others from entering it at all. In recognizing and ending marriages that were void from the beginning, Article 36 of the Family Code affirms the social and human value of marriage as an institution meant to elevate, not diminish, the lives of those who enter into it.[^299]
   * *Fidelity:* The inquiry does not turn on a numerical count of affairs. In [*Quiogue, Jr. v. Quiogue*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68704), the Supreme Court stated:

Under Article 68 of the Family Code, the "husband and wife are obliged to live together, observe mutual love, respect and fidelity, and render mutual help and support." Clearly, the law itself recognizes fidelity as the norm and a spouse should not be made to settle for anything less than absolute faithfulness from the other. This stems from the nature of marriage being a "special contract" of an exclusive partnership between a man and a woman. While it is true that infidelity is a ground for legal separation, the same may also be an indication of a psychological incapacity if, for the same reason, one is completely unable to discharge the essential obligations of marriage.

Of course, this is not to say that the Court will intuitively declare a marriage void for a single act of marital indiscretion. Infidelity is not measured in terms of frequency. To be considered as a form of psychological incapacity, infidelity must satisfy the requirements of

(1) gravity or severity,

(2) antecedence, and

(3) legal incurability or persistence during the marriage.

(1) Juridical antecedence:

In this case, Antonio's Chronic infidelity is not only comprised of multiple illicit amorous relations. As detailed in the Psychiatric Evaluation by Dr. Garcia, his affairs are not casual mistakes as these were shown to be deeply rooted in his psychopathology which was in place even before his marriage.[^300]
   * *Mutual help and support:* Mutual help and support extend beyond pecuniary maintenance to emotional, moral, and psychological solidarity during crises, illness, childbirth, and family hardship.[^301] [^302] [^303] [^304] 
2. **Article 69: Family domicile:** The statutory duty must be distinguished from the evidence of incapacity in an individual case. The Family Code provides:

Art. 69. The husband and wife shall fix the family domicile. In case of disagreement, the court shall decide.

The court may exempt one spouse from living with the other if the latter should live abroad or there are other valid and compelling reasons for the exemption. However, such exemption shall not apply if the same is not compatible with the solidarity of the family. (110a)[^305]
3. **Article 70 (Joint Support of the Family):** The spouses are jointly responsible for the support of the family, with expenses met from the community property, income or fruits of separate properties, or separate properties themselves.[^306] [^307]  Chronic, parasitic refusal to contribute to family support despite capacity, or the compulsive dissipation of family resources on drugs, gambling, or vices while leaving the family destitute, violates Article 70, as established in [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), [*Alberto v. Alberto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68353) (2022), [*Mutya-Sumilhig v. Sumilhig*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68705) (2022), and *Alfonso v. Alfonso* (2025).[^308] [^309] [^310] [^311] [^312] 
4. **Article 71 (Management of the Household):** The management of the household shall be the right and the duty of both spouses.[^313] [^314] [^315] 

### 6.2 Parental Obligations Toward Children (Articles 220, 221, and 225) as Essential Marital Duties

In [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), the Supreme Court carefully examined why parental duties under Articles 220, 221, and 225 of the Family Code (keeping children in their company, supporting and educating them, giving them love and affection, advice and counsel, companionship and understanding, providing moral and spiritual guidance, and protecting them from bad company and destructive habits) are relevant to Article 36.[^316] [^317] 

The Court noted a nuance: strictly speaking, parental authority and duties attach to the parent-child relationship regardless of whether the parents are married, and a childless couple still has essential marital obligations.[^318]  Nevertheless, because Article 1 of the Family Code defines marriage as a union entered into for the establishment of conjugal and family life, when a couple has children, the joint nurturing, protection, and rearing of those children become integral to the marital consortium.[^319]  [^320]  Thus, in [*Kalaw v. Fernandez*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/59147) (2015) (habitually leaving young children unattended to play mahjong), [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021) (exposing the couple's young daughter to a room thick with marijuana and *shabu* smoke and failing to care for her during hospitalizations), [*Quilpan v. Quilpan*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67927) (2021), [*Alberto v. Alberto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68353) (2022), [*Republic v. Ramoran-Wong*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70390) (2026) (neglecting and becoming apathetic toward the couple's young son), and [*Soto v. Reyes-Soto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70372) (2026), a spouse's gross, enduring inability to perform fundamental parental and family duties serves as powerful proof of psychological incapacity to establish a family life.[^321] [^322] [^323] [^324] [^325] [^326] 

---

## 7. Evidentiary Standards, Burden of Proof, and Forensic Methodology

### 7.1 Quantum of Proof: Clear and Convincing Evidence

Prior to [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), civil petitions under Article 36 were nominally governed by the general civil standard of preponderance of evidence under Rule 133, Section 1 of the Rules of Court, even as *Molina* Guideline No. 1 instructed that any doubt should be resolved in favor of the existence and continuation of the marriage.[^327] [^328] [^329]  In [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507), the Supreme Court *En Banc* resolved this doctrinal ambiguity by holding that **the plaintiff-spouse must prove their case with clear and convincing evidence**.[^330] 

The Court explained why **clear and convincing evidence** is the exact quantum required:[^331] 

* **Why preponderance is insufficient:** Marriage enjoys a strong constitutional and statutory presumption of validity (*semper praesumitur pro matrimonio*).[^const-1987]  [^332]  Across Philippine evidence law, whenever a party seeks to overcome a strong legal presumption (such as the presumption of regularity of official acts, the validity of a notarized public instrument, or the absence of fraud), our jurisprudence requires *clear and convincing evidence*, not mere preponderance.[^333] 
* **The governing quantum:** The Supreme Court stated in [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507):

Molina, however, is silent on what quantum of proof is required in nullity cases. While there is opinion that a nullity case under Article 36 is like any civil case that requires preponderance of evidence, we now hold that the plaintiff-spouse must prove his or her case with clear and convincing evidence. This is a quantum of proof that requires more than preponderant evidence but less than proof beyond reasonable doubt.

The reason is that this jurisdiction follows the presumption of validity of marriages. As was held in the 1922 case of Adong v. Cheong Seng Gee:

The basis of human society throughout the civilized world is that of marriage. Marriage in this jurisdiction is not only a civil contract, but it is a new relation, an institution in the maintenance of which the public is deeply interested. Consequently, every internment of the law leans toward legalizing matrimony. Persons dwelling together in apparent matrimony are presumed, in the absence of any counter-presumption or evidence special to the case, to be in fact married. The reason is that such is the common order of society, and if the parties were not what they thus hold themselves out as being, they would be living in the constant violation of decency and of law. A presumption established by our Code of Civil Procedure is "that a man and woman deporting themselves as husband and wife have entered into a lawful contract of marriage." (Sec. 334, No. 28.) Semper praesumitur pro matrimonio — Always presume marriage. (Citation omitted)

As with any presumption—such as the presumption of regularity in the issuance of public documents, regularity in the performance of duty, of good faith, or of sufficient consideration—it can only be rebutted with clear and convincing evidence.[^334]
* **Totality of evidence test:** In evaluating whether clear and convincing evidence has been presented, courts apply the **totality of evidence** standard first articulated in [*Marcos v. Marcos*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/38116) (2000) and reaffirmed in [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021), [*Alberto v. Alberto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68353) (2022), [*Kusk v. Kusk*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70120) (2025), [*Soto v. Reyes-Soto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70372) (2026), and [*Republic v. Ramoran-Wong*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70390) (2026): no single piece of evidence is dispositive in isolation; rather, the court weighs the cumulative force of the petitioner's testimony, corroborating lay witnesses, pre-marital history, documentary exhibits, and (when offered) psychological or psychiatric evaluations.[^335] [^336] [^337] [^338] [^339] [^340] 

### 7.2 Lay Witness Testimony and Pre-Marital Lived Experiences

Because [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) abolished the requirement of expert psychiatric testimony, **ordinary lay witnesses** now stand at the center of Article 36 fact-finding.[^341] [^342] [^343]  However, the Supreme Court has carefully delineated what makes lay testimony probative versus insufficient:

* **Ordinary witnesses:** The Supreme Court explained in [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507):

In light of the foregoing, this Court now categorically abandons the second Molina guideline. Psychological incapacity is neither a mental incapacity nor a personality disorder that must be proven through expert opinion. There must be proof, however, of the durable or enduring aspects of a person's personality, called "personality structure," which manifests itself through clear acts of dysfunctionality that undermines the family. The spouse's personality structure must make it impossible for him or her to understand and, more important, to comply with his or her essential marital obligations.

Proof of these aspects of personality need not be given by an expert. Ordinary witnesses who have been present in the life of the spouses before the latter contracted marriage may testify on behaviors that they have consistently observed from the supposedly incapacitated spouse. From there, the judge will decide if these behaviors are indicative of a true and serious incapacity to assume the essential marital obligations.

In this way, the Code Committee's intent to limit the incapacity to "psychic causes" is fulfilled. Furthermore, there will be no need to label a person as having a mental disorder just to obtain a decree of nullity. A psychologically incapacitated person need not be shamed and pathologized for what could have been a simple mistake in one's choice of intimate partner, a mistake too easy to make as when one sees through rose-colored glasses. A person's psychological incapacity to fulfill his or her marital obligations should not be at the expense of one's dignity, because it could very well be that he or she did not know that the incapacity existed in the first place.[^344]
* **Case-specific evaluation:** The evidence must be assessed on the actual record. In [*Clavecilla v. Clavecilla*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68752), the Supreme Court stated:

To reiterate, psychological incapacity does not simply connote difficulty, refusal, or neglect in performing marital obligations.
