As a general rule under Article 165 of the Family Code, children conceived and born outside a valid marriage are illegitimate.[^477]  However, **Article 54 of the Family Code** carves out an explicit statutory exception for Article 36:[^478] 

* **Children conceived or born before the finality of the Article 36 judgment are legitimate:** Under Article 54, children conceived or born *before* the judgment of annulment or absolute nullity of the marriage under Article 36 has become final and executory **shall be considered legitimate**.[^479] [^480] [^481] 
* **A child born before the wedding:** The effect of an Article 36 judgment on an already legitimated child was decided in [*Republic v. Tangarorang*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69852). The Supreme Court stated:

At the outset, Article 165 of the Family Code provides that "children conceived and born outside a valid marriage are illegitimate, unless otherwise provided in the Family Code." However, children may be legitimated by a subsequent valid marriage of their parents under Articles 177 and 178 of the same Code, if their parents were not disqualified by any impediment to marry each other at the time of their conception.

Here, it is undisputed that respondents are the biological parents of Sharemahlyne. As shown in her Certificate of Live Birth, she was born on September 9, 2006 to respondents who were not disqualified by any impediment to marry each other. While psychological incapacity was later found to have rendered respondent Ramer incapable of fulfilling his essential marital obligations, such incapacity is not an impediment under the law that would disqualify him from contracting marriage. Thus, with their marriage taking effect on April 12, 2007, Sharemahlyne had been legitimated.

Considering that "a void marriage is deemed never to have taken place at all," the nullity of the marriage, as a general rule, will make the child's status illegitimate from conception. However, Article 54 of the Family Code provides for exceptions:

Art. 54. Children conceived or born before the judgment of annulment or absolute nullity of the marriage under Article 36 has become final and executory shall be considered legitimate. Children conceived or born of the subsequent marriage under Article 53 shall likewise be legitimate.

Based on the foregoing provision, there are two instances when children born outside of valid marriages would still be considered legitimate so that the declaration of nullity of marriage would not result in a change of their status:

(1) when the parent/s is/are declared psychologically incapacitated under Article 36 of the Family Code; and

(2) when the child is conceived or born of the subsequent marriage under Article 53 of the Family Code, but the parent/s has/have not complied with the requirements of Article 52 of the Family Code.

For void marriages under Article 53 of the Family Code, the language of Article 54 of the Family Code requires that children be conceived or born within the subsequent marriage to be considered legitimate. However, for void marriages under Article 36, Article 54 of the Family Code did not distinguish between a child born prior to the marriage and a child born during the subsisting marriage. All the provision requires for legitimacy is that the child must be conceived or born prior to the judgment of absolute nullity under Article 36.

It is an elementary rule in statutory construction that where the law does not distinguish, the courts should not distinguish. Relevantly, the lack of distinction between the class of legitimate children born in wedlock and the class of legitimated children born out of wedlock under Article 54, in relation to Article 36 of the Family Code, finds conformity with Article 179, which states that "legitimated children shall enjoy the same rights as legitimate children." In fact, the legitimate status of legitimate children retroacts to the time of their birth pursuant to Article 180 of the Family Code, further eliminating any perceived difference between legitimate and legitimated children. Indeed, legitimation "puts a legitimated child completely and fully on equal footing with children born in lawful wedlock."

It would also not be amiss to point out that the Family Code does not provide for a scenario where a legitimated child may revert to illegitimacy. This is in keeping with the principle that a legitimate status is more favorable to the child. Considering that "'in the eyes of the law, the legitimate child enjoys a preferred and superior status," the law protects the presumption of legitimacy, which is "based on the broad principles of natural justice and the supposed virtue of the mother. The presumption is grounded on the policy to protect innocent offspring from the odium of illegitimacy." It would thus be absurd to relegate children "to the status of illegitimacy, when they are already enjoying the rights accorded to legitimated children." To entertain such a situation would be anathema to the intent and purpose of the law in prioritizing the best interests of the child.

Considering the foregoing reasons, there is no substantial distinction between legitimate and legitimated children for purposes of determining the legitimacy of children of marriages later declared void under Article 36 of the Family Code. A declaration of nullity of marriage based on the psychological incapacity of one or both spouses under Article 36 should not affect the status of the children in accordance with Article 54 of the Family Code.

In the assailed Order, the RTC incorrectly ruled that Sharemahlyne remains to be an illegitimate child under Article 165 of the Family Code, as her legitimation was not reflected in her birth registry. Given that the legitimacy status of a child is conferred by the Family Code, a substantive law, the lack of annotation in the birth certificate as to the subsequent marriage of the parents of the child, shall not affect such status. Indeed, the formal requirement of annotating the legitimation is a mere administrative procedure which cannot impair substantive rights.

Accordingly, Article 54 of the Family Code entitled Sharemahlyne to retain her legitimacy status as a legal consequence of respondents' marriage. The RTC, in granting the petition for declaration of nullity of marriage on the ground of psychological incapacity, contravened the law in declaring her to be an illegitimate child as a consequence of the nullification.

Nevertheless, We recognize that Linney may have been impelled by a desire to claim sole custody of Sharemahlyne, as Ramer previously tried to keep their daughter away from her due to the VAWC case that she filed against him. It bears stressing however, that legitimacy is presumed to be more favorable to Sharemahlyne, and Article 54 in relation to Article 36 of the Family Code prescribes the child's legitimate status. Hence, the more appropriate action for the mother under these circumstances would be to file a separate petition to seek sole custody of her daughter.

All told, the RTC rulings were bereft of basis in declaring Sharemahlyne to be illegitimate. Since her legitimacy status is not affected by the declaration of nullity of marriage on the ground of psychological incapacity, the RTC must be reversed insofar as it declared Sharemahlyne to be an illegitimate child of respondents.

ACCORDINGLY, the Petition for Review on Certiorari is GRANTED. The August 29, 2023 Decision of Branch 1, Regional Trial Court, Butuan City in Civil Case No. 2021-0141 is AFFIRMED with MODIFICATION. Sharemahlyne Librada Tangarorang is DECLARED a legitimate child of respondents Linney Jean L. Tangarorang and Ramer R. Tangarorang, pursuant to Article 54 of the Family Code.[^482]

Further, the February 7, 2024 Order of the Regional Trial Court is REVERSED and SET ASIDE.

SO ORDERED.[^483]

### 10.3 Custody and Parental Authority Over Common Children (Article 213)

Because children of a marriage declared void under Article 36 are legitimate under Article 54, joint parental authority initially pertains to both parents under Article 211, and upon separation or nullity of marriage, **Article 213 of the Family Code** governs custody:[^484] [^485] [^486] [^487] 

* **Best interest of the child and the tender-age presumption:** Under Article 213, in case of separation of the parents, parental authority shall be exercised by the parent designated by the Court, taking into account all relevant considerations, especially the choice of the child over seven years of age, unless the parent chosen is unfit; furthermore, **no child under seven years of age shall be separated from the mother, unless the court finds compelling reasons to order otherwise**.[^488] [^489] [^490] 
* **Psychological incapacity does not automatically disqualify a parent from custody, unless the incapacity endangers the child:** In [*Tan-Andal v. Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (2021) and [*Alberto v. Alberto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68353) (2022), the Court explained that a finding of psychological incapacity toward *spousal* obligations does not *ipso facto* strip a parent of custody if they remain a fit parent; however, where the incapacitated spouse's personality structure directly endangers or neglects the children, such as Mario Andal's exposure of his daughter to drug use in [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) (where custody was awarded to the mother Rosanna), or the husband's abandonment and irresponsibility in [*Alberto v. Alberto*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68353) (2022), sole custody is properly awarded to the fit custodial spouse.[^491] [^492] 

### 10.4 Moral, Exemplary, and Nominal Damages (*Buenaventura v. Court of Appeals*)

Damages require their own factual basis. The First Division decided [*Buenaventura v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/42809), where the Supreme Court explained the inconsistency in treating the same acts as both involuntary incapacity and willful misconduct.[^493]

The award by the trial court of moral damages is based on Articles 2217 and 21 of the Civil Code, which read as follows:

ART. 2217. Moral damages include physical suffering, mental anguish, fright, serious anxiety, besmirched reputation, wounded feelings, moral shock, social humiliation, and similar injury. Though incapable of pecuniary computation, moral damages may be recovered if they are the proximate result of the defendant’s wrongful act or omission.

ART. 21. Any person who wilfully causes loss or injury to another in a manner that is contrary to morals, good customs or public policy shall compensate the latter for the damage.
The trial court referred to Article 21 because Article 2219 of the Civil Code enumerates the cases in which moral damages may be recovered and it mentions Article 21 as one of the instances. It must be noted that Article 21 states that the individual must willfully cause loss or injury to another. There is a need that the act is willful and hence done in complete freedom. In granting moral damages, therefore, the trial court and the Court of Appeals could not but have assumed that the acts on which the moral damages were based were done willfully and freely, otherwise the grant of moral damages would have no leg to stand on.

On the other hand, the trial court declared the marriage of the parties null and void based on Article 36 of the Family Code, due to psychological incapacity of the petitioner, Noel Buenaventura. Article 36 of the Family Code states:

A marriage contracted by any party who, at the time of the celebration, was psychologically incapacitated to comply with the essential marital obligations of marriage, shall likewise be void even if such incapacity becomes manifest only after its solemnization.

Psychological incapacity has been defined, thus:

. . . no less than a mental (not physical) incapacity that causes a party to be truly incognitive of the basic marital covenants that concomitantly must be assumed and discharged by the parties to the marriage which, as so expressed by Article 68 of the Family Code, include their mutual obligations to live together, observe love, respect and fidelity and render help and support. There is hardly any doubt that the intendment of the law has been to confine the meaning of "psychological incapacity" to the most serious cases of personality disorders clearly demonstrative of an utter insensitivity or inability to give meaning and significance to the marriage. . . .
The Court of Appeals and the trial court considered the acts of the petitioner after the marriage as proof of his psychological incapacity, and therefore a product of his incapacity or inability to comply with the essential obligations of marriage. Nevertheless, said courts considered these acts as willful and hence as grounds for granting moral damages. It is contradictory to characterize acts as a product of psychological incapacity, and hence beyond the control of the party because of an innate inability, while at the same time considering the same set of acts as willful. By declaring the petitioner as psychologically incapacitated, the possibility of awarding moral damages on the same set of facts was negated. The award of moral damages should be predicated, not on the mere act of entering into the marriage, but on specific evidence that it was done deliberately and with malice by a party who had knowledge of his or her disability and yet willfully concealed the same. No such evidence appears to have been adduced in this case.

For the same reason, since psychological incapacity means that one is truly incognitive of the basic marital covenants that one must assume and discharge as a consequence of marriage, it removes the basis for the contention that the petitioner purposely deceived the private respondent. If the private respondent was deceived, it was not due to a willful act on the part of the petitioner. Therefore, the award of moral damages was without basis in law and in fact.

Since the grant of moral damages was not proper, it follows that the grant of exemplary damages cannot stand since the Civil Code provides that exemplary damages are imposed in addition to moral, temperate, liquidated or compensatory damages.[^494]

---

## 11. Procedural Architecture: A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC, Res Judicata, and Appellate Review

### 11.1 Standing and Exclusivity of Direct Petitions (A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC; *Bolos*; *Fujiki*; *Juliano-Llave*)

Effective March 15, 2003, the Supreme Court promulgated A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC (*Rule on Declaration of Absolute Nullity of Void Marriages and Annulment of Voidable Marriages*). Section 2(a) thereof provides that a petition for declaration of absolute nullity of a void marriage may be filed **solely by the husband or the wife**.[^495] [^496] 

The Supreme Court has delineated the scope of this standing rule across three distinct contexts:

1. **Inapplicability of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC to marriages celebrated before the Family Code ([*Bolos v. Bolos*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/54656)):** In [*Bolos v. Bolos*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/54656) (2010), the Court held that the phrase under the Family Code in Section 1 of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC restricts the procedural rule's application to marriages celebrated on or after August 3, 1988 (the effectivity of the Family Code), although on substantive grounds Article 36 itself applies retroactively under Article 256 to pre-Family Code marriages.[^497] [^498] 
2. **Prior spouse's standing to assail a subsequent bigamous marriage ([*Fujiki v. Marinay*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/55916)):** In [*Fujiki v. Marinay*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/55916) (2013), the Court clarified that Section 2(a) of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC applies only where the petitioner is one of the spouses in a void marriage (such as under Article 36); in a bigamous marriage under Article 35(4), the aggrieved prior spouse of the first subsisting marriage has standing to file a petition for declaration of nullity (or recognition of foreign judgment of nullity under Rule 108) because the prior spouse's marital status and property rights are directly injured.[^499] 
3. **Collateral attack by compulsory heirs after the death of a spouse:** While a *direct* petition for declaration of nullity under A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC can only be filed by the husband or the wife during their lifetime (and the proceeding is closed and terminated if a party dies before entry of judgment under Section 24), compulsory or intestate heirs may *collaterally* attack the validity of a void marriage after the death of the spouse in a proceeding for the settlement of the decedent's estate to determine heirship and successional rights.[^500] [^501] 

### 11.2 Pleading Requirements, Role of the State, and Anti-Collusion Safeguards

Under A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC and Supreme Court jurisprudence:

* **Ultimate facts vs. evidentiary details ([*Barcelona v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/47681); [*Salita v. Magtolis*](https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri1994/jun1994/gr_106429_1994.html)):** Section 2(d) of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC requires the petition to allege the complete facts showing that either or both parties were psychologically incapacitated from complying with the essential marital obligations of marriage at the time of celebration, including the physical manifestations thereof, without need of alleging expert opinion.[^502] [^503]  In [*Barcelona v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/47681) (2003), the Court held that a petition is sufficient if it states the ultimate facts of the incapacity and the non-complied-with marital obligations; evidentiary details are matters of proof at trial.[^504] 
* **Mandatory investigation against collusion ([*Republic v. Cuison-Melgar*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/40740); [*Mendoza v. Republic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/55310)):** Under Article 48 of the Family Code and Section 9 of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC, where the respondent fails to file an answer or does not actively oppose the petition, the trial court must order the public prosecutor to investigate and submit a written collusion report on whether the parties are colluding to obtain a decree of nullity.[^505] [^506] [^507]  However, where the public prosecutor actively participates in the trial, cross-examines the petitioner's witnesses, and the respondent vigorously contests the petition (as in *Tuason v. Court of Appeals* and [*Marcos v. Marcos*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/38116)), the absence of a formal collusion report does not nullify the proceedings because active adversarial litigation negates collusion.[^508] [^509] 
* **Withdrawal after a counterclaim has been pleaded:** The applicable paragraph of Rule 17 matters. In [*Blay v. Baña*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/63981), the Supreme Court applied Section 2, not dismissal by notice under Section 1:

Section 2, Rule 17 of the Rules of Court provides for the procedure relative to counterclaims in the event that a complaint is dismissed by the court at the plaintiffs instance, viz.:

Section 2. Dismissal upon motion of plaintiff. — Except as provided in the preceding section, a complaint shall not be dismissed at the plaintiff's instance save upon approval of the court and upon such terms and conditions as the court deems proper. If a counterclaim has been pleaded by a defendant prior to the service upon him of the plaintiff's motion for dismissal, the dismissal shall be limited to the complaint. The dismissal shall be without prejudice to the right of the defendant to prosecute his counterclaim in a separate action unless within fifteen

(15) days from notice of the motion he manifests his preference to have his counterclaim resolved in the same action. Unless otherwise specified in the order, a dismissal under this paragraph shall be without prejudice. A class suit shall not be dismissed or compromised without the approval of the court.

As per the second sentence of the provision, if a counterclaim has been pleaded by the defendant prior to the service upon him of the plaintiff's motion for the dismissal - as in this case - the rule is that the dismissal shall be limited to the complaint. Commentaries on the subject elucidate that "instead of an 'action' shall not be dismissed, the present rule uses the term 'complaint'. A dismissal of an action is different from a mere dismissal of the complaint. For this reason, since only the complaint and not the action is dismissed, the defendant inspite of said dismissal may still prosecute his counterclaim in the same action.

However, as stated in the third sentence of Section 2, Rule 17, if the defendant desires to prosecute his counterclaim in the same action, he is required to file a manifestation within fifteen

(15) days from notice othe motion. Otherwise, his counterclaim may be prosecuted in a separate action. As explained by renowned remedial law expert, former Associate Justice Florenz D. Regalado, in his treatise on the matter:

Under this revised section, where the plaintiff moves for the dismissal of the complaint to which a counterclaim has been interpose, the dismissal shall be limited to the complaint. Such dismissal shall be without prejudice to the right of the defendant to either prosecute his counterclaim in a separate action or to have the same resolved in the same action. Should he opt for the first alternative, the court should render the corresponding order granting and reserving his right to prosecute his claim in a separate complaint. Should he choose to have his counterclaim disposed of in the same action wherein the complaint had been dismissed, he must manifest within 15 days from notice to him of plaintiff's motion to dismiss. 

In this case, the CA confined the application of Section 2, Rule 17 to that portion of its second sentence which states that the "dismissal shall be limited to the complaint." Evidently, the CA ignored the same provision's third sentence, which provides for the alternatives available to the defendant who interposes a counterclaim prior to the service upon him of the plaintiff’s motion for dismissal. As may be clearly inferred therefrom, should the defendant desire to prosecute his counterclaim, he is required to manifest his preference therefor within fifteen

(15) days from notice of the plaintiffs motion to dismiss. Failing in which, the counterclaim may be prosecuted only in a separate action.

The rationale behind this rule is not difficult to discern: the passing of the fifteen (15)-day period triggers the finality of the court's dismissal of the complaint and hence, bars the conduct of further proceedings, i.e., the prosecution of respondent's counterclaim, in the same action. Thus, in order to obviate this finality, the defendant is required to file the required manifestation within the aforesaid period; otherwise, the counterclaim may be prosecuted only in a separate action.

It is hornbook doctrine in statutory construction that "the whole and every part of the statute must be considered in fixing the meaning of any of its parts and in order to produce a harmonious whole. A statute must be so construed as to harmonize and give effect to all its provisions whenever possible. In short, every meaning to be given to each word or phrase must be ascertained from the context of the body of the statute since a word or phrase in a statute is always used in association with other words or phrases and its meaning may be modified or restricted by the latter."

By narrowly reading Section 2, Rule 17 of the Rules of Court, the CA clearly violated the foregoing principle and in so doing, erroneously sustained the assailed RTC Orders declaring respondent's counterclaim "as remaining for independent adjudication" despite the latter's failure to file the required manifestation within the prescribed fifteen (15)-day period. As petitioner aptly points out:

If the intention of the framers of the Rules of Court is a blanket dismissal of the complaint ALONE if a counterclaim has been pleaded prior to the service of the notice of dismissal then there is NO EVIDENT PURPOSE for the third (3rd) sentence of Sec. 2, Rule 17.

It is clearly an ABSURD conclusion if the said provision will direct the defendant to manifest within fifteen

(15) days from receipt of the notice of dismissal his preference to prosecute his counterclaim in the SAME ACTION when the same AUTOMATICALLY REMAINS. If the automatic survival of the counterclaim and the death of the complaint as being ruled by the Court of Appeals in its questioned Decision is indeed true, then the third sentence should have required defendant to manifest that he will prosecute his counterclaim in a SEPARATE and not as the provision reads - in the same ACTION. (Emphases and underscoring in the original)

Petitioner's observations are logically on point. Consequently, the CA rulings, which affirmed the patently erroneous RTC Orders, must be reversed. As it should be, the RTC should have only granted petitioner's Motion to Withdraw and hence, dismissed his Petition for Declaration of Nullity of Marriage, without prejudice to, among others, the prosecution of respondent's counterclaim in a separate action.

WHEREFORE, the petition is GRANTED. The Decision dated February 23, 2017 and the Resolution dated June 6, 2017 of the Court of Appeals in CA-G.R. SP No. 146138 are hereby REVERSED and SET ASIDE. A new one is ENTERED solely granting petitioner Alex Raul B. Blay's Motion to Withdraw his Petition for Declaration of Nullity of Marriage in Civil Case No. R-PSY-14-17714-CV. The aforesaid dismissal is, among others, without prejudice to the prosecution of respondent Cynthia B. Baña's counterclaim in a separate action.[^510]

### 11.3 Res Judicata and Splitting a Single Cause of Action (*Mallion v. Alcantara*)

Suppose a spouse files a petition for declaration of nullity of marriage based solely on Article 36 (psychological incapacity), loses by final judgment, and subsequently files a second petition for declaration of nullity of the *same* marriage based on Article 35(3) (absence of a valid marriage license). Is the second petition barred by *res judicata*?

In [*Mallion v. Alcantara*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/42362) (2006), the Supreme Court held that the second petition **is barred by *res judicata* and the rule against splitting a single cause of action** (Rule 2, Section 4 of the Rules of Court).[^511]  The Court explained that in actions for declaration of absolute nullity of marriage, the single cause of action is the *nullity of the marriage itself*, and all legal grounds for nullity existing at the time of the first suit (whether absence of a marriage license under Article 35 or psychological incapacity under Article 36) are merely alternative predicates for that single cause of action.[^512]  By invoking only Article 36 in the first suit and omitting the alleged lack of a marriage license, the petitioner implicitly admitted that the marriage had all formal requisites and waived all other existing grounds that could have been raised.[^513] [^514] 

### 11.4 Finality, Entry of Judgment, Civil Registry Registration, and Remarriage (Articles 40, 52, and 53)

Under Sections 19 to 23 of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC and Articles 40, 50, 52, and 53 of the Family Code:[^515] [^516] [^517] [^518] 

1. **Finality of Decision:** The decision declaring the marriage void under Article 36 becomes final upon the expiration of fifteen days from notice to the parties (including the OSG) if no motion for reconsideration or appeal is filed.[^519] 
2. **Registration of Entry of Judgment and Decree of Absolute Nullity:** Upon finality, Entry of Judgment is issued and registered in the Civil Registry where the marriage was recorded and in the Civil Registry where the Family Court granting the petition is located; only after registration of the Entry of Judgment does the court issue the **Decree of Absolute Nullity**, which is likewise registered in the proper civil registries.[^520] [^521] 
3. **Capacity to Remarry:** Under Article 40, the absolute nullity of a previous marriage may be invoked *for purposes of remarriage* on the basis solely of a final judgment declaring such previous marriage void.[^522] [^523]  As clarified in [*Diño v. Diño*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/33909) (2011), because Article 147 governs the property relations of an Article 36 marriage, the issuance of the Decree of Absolute Nullity in an Article 36 case is *not* conditioned on the prior liquidation and partition of co-owned properties or delivery of presumptive legitimes under Articles 50–52 (which apply only to Article 40 and Article 45 marriages).[^524] [^525] [^526] [^527] 

---

## 12. Criminal Law, Bigamy, and Conflict-of-Laws Intersections

### 12.1 Bigamy (Art. 349, Revised Penal Code), Article 40, and the *Pulido v. People* Abandonment of *Mercado* and *Tenebro*

For over two decades (under cases such as [*Mercado v. Tan*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/37964) (2000), [*Beltran v. People*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/37563) (2000), [*Ty v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/38264) (2000), [*Tenebro v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/47053) (2004), *Jarillo v. People*, [*Montañez v. Cipriano*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/55335) (2012), [*Pimentel v. Pimentel*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/54558) (2010), and [*Tilar v. Republic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/63283) (2017)), the Supreme Court enforced a draconian criminal rule on bigamy under Article 349 of the Revised Penal Code in relation to Article 40 of the Family Code:[^revised-penal-code] [^529] [^530] [^531] [^532] [^533] [^534] 

* **The old [*Mercado*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/37964) / [*Tenebro*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/47053) rule (2000–2021):** In [*Mercado v. Tan*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/37964) (2000), the Court held that a person who contracts a second marriage *before* obtaining a final judicial declaration of nullity of their first marriage under Article 40 commits the crime of bigamy under Article 349 of the Revised Penal Code, **even if the first marriage is subsequently declared void *ab initio* under Article 36**.[^revised-penal-code] [^535] [^536]  Worse still, in [*Tenebro v. Court of Appeals*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/47053) (2004), a divided Court held that an accused could be convicted of bigamy **even when the second (bigamous) marriage was subsequently declared void *ab initio* under Article 36** for psychological incapacity, on the theory that a marriage void under Article 36 still produced civil effects and fulfilled the elements of Article 349 at the moment of the wedding.[^537]  And in [*Beltran v. People*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/37563) (2000) and [*Pimentel v. Pimentel*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/54558) (2010), the Court held that a pending civil action for declaration of nullity of marriage under Article 36 did not constitute a prejudicial question to suspend a criminal prosecution for bigamy or frustrated parricide.[^538] [^539] 
The later ruling must be distinguished from the precedents it abandoned. In [*Pulido v. People*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67720), the Supreme Court stated:

> All told, we hold that in criminal prosecutions for bigamy, the accused can validly interpose the defense of a void ab initio marriage even without obtaining a judicial declaration of absolute nullity. Consequently, a judicial declaration of absolute nullity of the first and/or subsequent marriages obtained by the accused in a separate proceeding, irrespective of the time within which they are secured, is a valid defense in the criminal prosecution for bigamy.[^540]

The particular defense based on the second marriage was addressed separately:

Needless to say, as to the absolute nullity of his second marriage with Baleda, it was declared void ab initio because of being bigamous and not because it lacked any of the essential requisites of a marriage. Hence, petitioner cannot use the same as a defense in his prosecution for bigamy.

Summary:

To summarize and for future guidance, the parties are not required to obtain a judicial declaration of absolute nullity of a void ab initio first and subsequent marriages in order to raise it as a defense in a bigamy case. The same rule now applies to all marriages celebrated under the Civil Code and the Family Code. Article 40 of the Family Code did not amend Article 349 of the RPC, and thus, did not deny the accused the right to collaterally attack the validity of a void ab initio marriage in the criminal prosecution for bigamy.

However, if the first marriage is merely voidable, the accused cannot interpose an annulment decree as a defense in the criminal prosecution for bigamy since the voidable first marriage is considered valid and subsisting when the second marriage was contracted. The crime of bigamy, therefore, is consummated when the second marriage was celebrated during the subsistence of the voidable first marriage. The same rule applies if the second marriage is merely considered as voidable.

To our mind, it is time to abandon the earlier precedents and adopt a more liberal view that a void ab initio marriage can be used as a defense in bigamy even without a separate judicial declaration of absolute nullity. The accused may present testimonial or documentary evidence such as the judicial declaration of absolute nullity of the first and/or subsequent void ab initio marriages in the criminal prosecution for bigamy. The said view is more in accord with the retroactive effects of a void ab initio marriage, the purpose of and legislative intent behind Article 40 of the Family Code, and the rule on statutory construction of penal laws. Therefore, the absence of a "prior valid marriage" and the subsequent judicial declaration of absolute nullity of his first marriage, Pulido is hereby acquitted from the crime of Bigamy charged against him.

WHEREFORE, the Petition for Review on Certiorari is hereby GRANTED. The March 17, 2015 Decision and August 18, 2015 Resolution of the Court of Appeals in CA-G.R. CR No. 33008 are hereby REVERSED and SET ASIDE. Petitioner Luisito G. Pulido is ACQUITTED.[^541]



### 12.2 Transnational Marriages, Foreign Spouses, Article 26(2), and Conflict of Laws

When an Article 36 petition involves a foreign national or a marriage celebrated abroad, several conflict-of-laws doctrines intersect with psychological incapacity:

1. **A foreign spouse and Philippine proceedings:** A foreign nationality does not by itself dispose of a petition. In [*Ambrose v. Suque-Ambrose*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67590), the Supreme Court ordered:

WHEREFORE, in view of the foregoing, the instant petition for review on certiorari is hereby GRANTED. Consequently, the Decision dated February 13, 2013 and Order dated April 8, 2013 of the Regional Trial Court (RTC) of Quezon City Branch 89, in Civil Case No. Q-07-60216 are REVERSED and SET ASIDE. The case is hereby REMANDED to the RTC for further proceedings and judgment on the merits.

SO ORDERED.[^542]

The actual place of celebration must also be kept distinct. In [*Kusk v. Kusk*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70120), Milagrosa went to Denmark and the parties married in November 1992; the spouses moved to the Philippines in July 1993.[^543]
2. **Cultural differences vs. psychological incapacity ([*Republic v. Quintero-Hamano*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/46281); [*Republic v. Tecag*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/64764); [*Kusk v. Kusk*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70120)):** In mixed marriages between a Filipino and a foreign national, the Court in [*Republic v. Quintero-Hamano*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/46281) (2004) (involving a Japanese husband who abandoned his Filipina wife and child) held that the requirements of Article 36 do not relax merely because the respondent is a foreigner; cultural differences or abandonment by a foreign spouse do not automatically prove psychological incapacity without evidence of a grave, antecedent personality structure.[^544] [^545]  Conversely, where clear and convincing evidence establishes that either the Filipino spouse or the foreign spouse suffers from an antecedent, grave, and legally incurable personality structure, as in [*Green v. Green*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69822) (2025) and [*Kusk v. Kusk*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/70120) (2025), Philippine courts readily grant nullity under Article 36.[^546] [^547] 
3. **Relationship to Article 26(2) Foreign Divorce ([*Republic v. Manalo*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/64093); [*Dela Cruz-Lanuza v. Lanuza, Jr.*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69467); [*Asilo v. Gonzales-Betic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69515)):** Under Article 26, paragraph 2 of the Family Code, as authoritatively interpreted in [*Republic v. Manalo*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/64093) (2018), [*Dela Cruz-Lanuza v. Lanuza, Jr.*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69467) (2024), and [*Asilo v. Gonzales-Betic*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/69515) (2024), where a marriage between a Filipino citizen and a foreigner is validly celebrated and a divorce is thereafter validly obtained abroad by *either* the alien spouse *or* the Filipino spouse capacitating them to remarry, the Filipino spouse shall likewise have capacity to remarry under Philippine law upon judicial recognition of the foreign divorce decree and proof of the foreign national law.[^548] [^549] [^550] [^551]  Consequently, in mixed marriages where a valid foreign divorce has already been obtained abroad, judicial recognition of foreign divorce under Article 26(2) and Rule 108 provides a direct statutory remedy without needing to litigate psychological incapacity under Article 36; where no foreign divorce has been obtained, however, either spouse may prosecute a petition for declaration of nullity under Article 36 in the Philippines.[^552] [^553] [^554] [^555]

---

## 13. Jurisprudential Survey: Selected Cases (1994–2026)

To compare the Supreme Court's treatment of the selected factual patterns, the following chronological survey synthesizes the landmark and representative decisions from 1994 through 2026.

### 13.1 The Formative and *Molina* Era (1994–2005)

* ***Salita v. Magtolis* (1994):** The Supreme Court sustained the sufficiency of the bill of particulars already filed, not a general exemption from providing one.[^556] Its ruling was:

We sustain the view of respondent Court of Appeals that the Bill of Particulars filed by private respondent is sufficient to state a cause of action, and to require more details from private respondent would be to ask for information on evidentiary matters. Indeed, petitioner has already been adequately apprised of private respondent’s cause of action against her thus —

. . . . (she) was psychologically incapacitated to comply with the essential marital obligations of their marriage in that she was unable to understand and accept the demands made by his profession — that of a newly qualified Doctor of Medicine — upon petitioner’s time and efforts so that she frequently complained of his lack of attention to her even to her mother, whose intervention caused petitioner to lose his job.

On the basis of the aforequoted allegations, it is evident that petitioner can already prepare her responsive pleading or for trial. Private respondent has already alleged that "she (petitioner) was unable to understand and accept the demands made by his profession . . . upon his time and efforts . . . " Certainly, she can respond to this. To demand for more details would indeed be asking for information on evidentiary facts — facts necessary to prove essential or ultimate facts.13 For sure, the additional facts called for by petitioner regarding her particular acts or omissions would be evidentiary, and to obtain evidentiary matters is not the function of a motion for bill of particulars.14[^557]
* ***Santos v. Court of Appeals* (1995):** Landmark *En Banc* decision defining the three essential characteristics of psychological incapacity (**gravity**, **juridical antecedence**, and **incurability**) derived from Canon 1095(3) of the New Code of Canon Law and the Family Code Revision Committee deliberations; denied nullity where a wife who went to the United States to work as a nurse failed to return home, although the decision records a January 1, 1989 telephone call, holding that mere abandonment without proof of an antecedent psychic disorder does not satisfy Article 36.[^558] 
* ***Valdes v. Regional Trial Court, Branch 102, Quezon City* (1996):** Established the governing doctrine on property relations in Article 36 cases: in a marriage declared void *ab initio* under Article 36, there is no Absolute Community of Property or Conjugal Partnership of Gains; instead, the property relations of the parties during cohabitation are governed by the special co-ownership regime under **Article 147 of the Family Code** (or Article 148 if an impediment exists), and Articles 50, 51, and 52 regarding liquidation of conjugal/community property do not apply to Article 36.[^559] 
* ***Chi Ming Tsoi v. Court of Appeals* (1997):** First Supreme Court decision affirming a declaration of nullity under Article 36; held that a husband's senseless, protracted refusal to have sexual intercourse with his wife throughout ten months of cohabitation, despite being physically and physiologically normal, demonstrated a grave, antecedent psychological incapacity to fulfill the essential marital obligations of procreation, mutual love, and intimacy.[^560] 
* ***Republic v. Court of Appeals and Molina* (1997):** Promulgated the famous eight mandatory guidelines governing Article 36 petitions, requiring inter alia that the root cause of psychological incapacity be medically or clinically identified, alleged in the complaint, proven by experts, and shown to be medically or clinically permanent or incurable; reversed the grant of nullity where the husband's financial irresponsibility, dependence on parents, and abandonment showed only refusal or difficulty rather than a clinical illness.[^561] 
* ***Hernandez v. Court of Appeals* (1999):** Denied nullity where the husband exhibited habitual alcoholism, chronic sexual infidelity, refusal to support the family, and physical violence; held that without expert proof of a pre-existing medical or clinical root cause at the time of marriage, these acts constitute grounds for legal separation under Article 55, not psychological incapacity under Article 36.[^562] 
* ***Beltran v. People* (2000) and *Mercado v. Tan* (2000):** Held (under the earlier rule that [*Pulido v. People*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67720) in 2021 abandoned) that a pending Article 36 civil action is not a prejudicial question in a bigamy prosecution and that contracting a second marriage before obtaining a final judicial declaration of nullity under Article 40 consummates bigamy even if the first marriage is later declared void under Article 36.[^563] [^564] 
* ***Marcos v. Marcos* (2000):** Clarified that a personal medical or psychological examination of the respondent spouse by a physician or psychologist is **not a *conditio sine qua non*** for a declaration of nullity under Article 36 if the **totality of evidence** presented is enough to sustain a finding of psychological incapacity; however, on the facts, the Court denied nullity because the totality of evidence showed only the husband's post-military-discharge unemployment, hot temper, and episodic physical violence.[^565] 
* ***Ty v. Court of Appeals* (2000):** Held that Article 36 applies retroactively under Article 256 to marriages celebrated before the Family Code, and reaffirmed that no damages may be recovered simply because a prior marriage was void for lack of a license.[^566] 
The merits and procedural results must be distinguished:

| Decision | Final disposition |
| :--- | :--- |
| [*Republic v. Dagdag*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/50120) | WHEREFORE, the present petition is GRANTED. The assailed Decision of the Court of Appeals dated April 22, 1993, in CA-G.R. CV No. 34378 is REVERSED and SET ASIDE.<br><br>No pronouncement as to costs.<br><br>SO ORDERED.[^567] |
| [*Malcampo-Sin v. Sin*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/50855) | WHEREFORE, the Court REVERSES and SETS ASIDE the appealed decision of the Court of Appeals in CA-G. R. CV No. 51304, promulgated on April 30, 1998 and the decision of the Regional Trial Court, Branch 158, Pasig City in Civil Case No. 3190, dated June 16, 1995.<br><br>Let the case be REMANDED to the trial court for proper trial.<br><br>No costs.<br><br>SO ORDERED.[^568] |
| [*Pesca v. Pesca*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/52118) | WHEREFORE, the herein petition is DENIED. No costs.<br><br>SO ORDERED.[^569] |
* ***Choa v. Choa* (2002):** Reversed a trial court order denying a demurrer to evidence; held that the husband's evidence, which complained mainly of the wife's filing of criminal and administrative suits against him and her alleged domineering character, utterly failed to establish any grave, antecedent, or incurable psychological incapacity.[^570] 
* ***Barcelona v. Court of Appeals* (2003):** Sustained the sufficiency of an Article 36 petition under A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC; held that procedural rules do not require the petition to plead evidentiary details or expert medical diagnoses, provided ultimate facts of the incapacity and non-complied-with obligations are stated.[^571] 
* ***Mercado-Fehr v. Fehr* (2003):** Affirmed the declaration of nullity under Article 36 and applied [*Valdes*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/33980), holding that a condominium acquired during the parties' cohabitation was governed by the special co-ownership regime of Article 147 (not ACP or CPG) and owned by them in equal shares.[^572] 
* ***Dedel v. Court of Appeals* (2004), *Republic v. Quintero-Hamano* (2004), *Carating-Siayngco v. Siayngco* (2004), and *Republic v. Iyoy* (2005):** Denied nullity across varied factual settings, including a wife's compulsive promiscuity ([*Dedel*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/46669)), a Japanese husband's abandonment of his Filipina family (*Quintero-Hamano*), mutual marital dissatisfaction after decades of cohabitation ([*Carating-Siayngco*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/46167)), and a Filipino wife's abandonment of her husband to obtain a foreign divorce and marry an American (*Iyoy*), holding that sexual infidelity, abandonment, and cultural differences do not constitute psychological incapacity absent proof of a grave, antecedent psychic disorder.[^573] [^574] [^575] [^576] 
* ***Tenebro v. Court of Appeals* (2004):** Held (within the line of rulings on second marriages that [*Pulido v. People*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/67720) in 2021 abandoned) that a subsequent judicial declaration of nullity of the second marriage on the ground of psychological incapacity under Article 36 does not extinguish criminal liability for bigamy.[^577] 
* ***Buenaventura v. Court of Appeals* (2005):** Established that moral and exemplary damages cannot be awarded against a spouse solely by reason of being declared psychologically incapacitated under Article 36, because psychological incapacity is an involuntary inability to understand and comply with marital obligations, which is antithetical to the willful malice or bad faith required for tort damages; also reaffirmed that Article 147 governs property division.[^578] 

### 13.2 The Transitional Era: *Antonio*, *Ngo Te*, *Diño*, and *Kalaw* (2006–2020)

* ***Antonio v. Reyes* (2006):** Landmark decision granting nullity where the wife was a persistent, pathological liar who fabricated an entire imaginary social, academic, and professional existence; held that such pervasive falsehoods precluded her from fulfilling the essential obligation of mutual love, respect, and trust under Article 68, and accorded persuasive weight to a parallel canonical nullity decree from the National Appellate Matrimonial Tribunal.[^579] 
* ***Yu v. Yu* (2006) and *Mallion v. Alcantara* (2006):** Addressed procedural bars and forum/cause-of-action rules; [*Mallion*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/42362) held that a final judgment denying a petition for nullity under Article 36 bars a subsequent petition for nullity of the same marriage based on lack of a marriage license under Article 35(3) under the doctrine of *res judicata* and the rule against splitting a single cause of action.[^580] [^581] 
* ***Republic v. Cuison-Melgar* (2006), *Perez-Ferraris v. Ferraris* (2006), *Paras v. Paras* (2007), *Tongol v. Tongol* (2007), and *Navales v. Navales* (2008):** Denied nullity where either no psychologist was presented and no collusion investigation was conducted (*Cuison-Melgar*), or where the psychological reports relied solely on the petitioner's one-sided narrative to label temper tantrums, infidelity, or career incompatibility as personality disorders.[^582] [^583] [^584] [^585] [^586] 
* ***Ngo Te v. Yu-Te* (2009):** Critiqued *Molina* as a strait-jacket that had contravened the Family Code Committee's intent and resulted in the near-universal denial of Article 36 petitions; granted nullity where a whirlwind marriage contracted under manipulative threats involved a wife with Antisocial Personality Disorder and a husband with Dependent Personality Disorder.[^587] 
* ***Ting v. Velez-Ting* (2009), *Padilla-Rumbaua v. Rumbaua* (2009), and *Cabacungan Alcazar v. Alcazar* (2009):** Reasserted the binding force of *Molina* pending *En Banc* modification and denied nullity where gambling, alcoholism, or abandonment after the wedding lacked independent proof of juridical antecedence.[^588] [^589] [^590] 
* ***Azcueta v. Republic* (2009) and *Camacho-Reyes v. Reyes* (2010):** Granted nullity where severe Dependent and Antisocial/Narcissistic Personality Disorders were established through comprehensive clinical evaluations and corroborative testimony showing that the husbands were pathologically dependent on their mothers or incapable of financial and emotional partnership from before the marriage.[^591] [^592] 
* ***Lim v. Sta. Cruz-Lim* (2010), *Suazo v. Suazo* (2010), *Toring v. Toring* (2010), and *Agraviador v. Amparo-Agraviador* (2010):** Denied nullity because the psychologists failed to link the respondents' post-marital flaws to pre-marital root causes through independent informants who knew the respondents before the wedding.[^593] [^594] [^595] [^596] 
* ***Bolos v. Bolos* (2010):** Held that A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC applies only to marriages celebrated during the effectivity of the Family Code (August 3, 1988 onward), even though Article 36 itself applies retroactively under Article 256.[^597] 
* ***Diño v. Diño* (2011):** Landmark ruling on decree issuance and property liquidation: held that Article 50 of the Family Code and Section 19(1) of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC (requiring prior liquidation, partition, and distribution of properties and delivery of presumptive legitimes before the issuance of the Decree of Absolute Nullity) apply **only to marriages declared void under Article 40 and voidable marriages under Article 45**, and **do not apply to marriages declared void under Article 36**, which are governed by co-ownership under Article 147.[^598] 
* ***Ochosa v. Alano* (2011), *Aurelio v. Aurelio* (2011), *Mendoza v. Republic* (2012), and *Republic v. De Quintos* (2012):** Denied nullity where the evidence established only post-marital sexual infidelity, in-law conflicts, or financial irresponsibility without proof of juridical antecedence.[^599] [^600] [^601] [^602] 
* ***Beumer v. Amores* (2012):** Held that a foreign husband whose marriage to a Filipina was declared void under Article 36 cannot invoke Article 147 co-ownership or unjust enrichment to claim a share or reimbursement over Philippine land, which is barred by Article XII, Section 7 of the 1987 Constitution.[^603] 
* ***Fujiki v. Marinay* (2013):** Clarified that Section 2(a) of A.M. No. 02-11-10-SC (limiting direct petitions to the husband or wife) does not prevent a prior legal spouse from filing a petition to recognize a foreign judgment declaring a subsequent bigamous marriage void.[^604] 
* ***Barrido v. Nonato* (2014):** Reaffirmed [*Valdes*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/33980) and [*Diño*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/33909), holding that properties acquired during a marriage declared void under Article 36 are governed by equal co-ownership under Article 147, regardless of which spouse held paper title.[^605] 
* ***Kalaw v. Fernandez* (2015):** On reconsideration of its 2011 decision, the Special First Division reversed itself and declared the marriage void under Article 36; held that the frequency and compulsiveness of the wife's mahjong gambling and habitual abandonment of her young children unattended demonstrated a grave, antecedent inability to fulfill maternal and conjugal duties, and cautioned against applying *Molina* as an unbending straitjacket.[^606] [^607]

### 13.3 The *Tan-Andal* and Post-*Tan-Andal* Era (2021–2026)

* ***Tan-Andal v. Andal* (May 11, 2021, *En Banc*):** Landmark decision penned by Associate Justice Marvic M.V.F. Leonen that fundamentally rewrote Article 36 jurisprudence.[^633]  Held that: (1) the quantum of proof in Article 36 cases is **clear and convincing evidence**; (2) psychological incapacity is **not a medical or mental illness**, but an enduring **personality structure** manifesting through clear acts of dysfunctionality that make it impossible to understand and comply with essential marital obligations; (3) **expert opinion from a psychologist or psychiatrist is no longer required**, as ordinary witnesses who observed the spouse's pre-marital life may prove the incapacity; (4) when an expert is presented, **personal examination of the respondent is not required**; (5) **incurability is legal, not medical**, meaning the incapacity is so enduring and persistent with respect to the specific partner that the couple's antagonistic personality structures inevitably cause the irreparable breakdown of the marriage; (6) Mario Andal's severe, adolescent-rooted drug addiction, financial irresponsibility, paranoia, and endangerment of his daughter satisfied gravity, juridical antecedence, and legal incurability; (7) Article 147 governed the property relations, but Rosanna exclusively owned her donated portion of the lot and Mario had no share in that lot or the duplex because the required contribution was absent; and (8) sole custody of their daughter was properly awarded to Rosanna.[^634] 
* ***Ambrose v. Suque-Ambrose* (June 23, 2021):** Held that a foreign citizen (an American citizen) who married a Filipina in the Philippines has legal standing and capacity to file a petition for declaration of nullity under Article 36 before a Philippine Family Court.[^635] 
The early post-[*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) decisions include both declarations of nullity and unsuccessful claims:

* ***Pulido v. People* (July 27, 2021, En Banc):** The Supreme Court stated:

All told, we hold that in criminal prosecutions for bigamy, the accused can validly interpose the defense of a void ab initio marriage even without obtaining a judicial declaration of absolute nullity. Consequently, a judicial declaration of absolute nullity of the first and/or subsequent marriages obtained by the accused in a separate proceeding, irrespective of the time within which they are secured, is a valid defense in the criminal prosecution for bigamy.[^644]

Needless to say, as to the absolute nullity of his second marriage with Baleda, it was declared void ab initio because of being bigamous and not because it lacked any of the essential requisites of a marriage. Hence, petitioner cannot use the same as a defense in his prosecution for bigamy.

Summary:

To summarize and for future guidance, the parties are not required to obtain a judicial declaration of absolute nullity of a void ab initio first and subsequent marriages in order to raise it as a defense in a bigamy case. The same rule now applies to all marriages celebrated under the Civil Code and the Family Code. Article 40 of the Family Code did not amend Article 349 of the RPC, and thus, did not deny the accused the right to collaterally attack the validity of a void ab initio marriage in the criminal prosecution for bigamy.

However, if the first marriage is merely voidable, the accused cannot interpose an annulment decree as a defense in the criminal prosecution for bigamy since the voidable first marriage is considered valid and subsisting when the second marriage was contracted. The crime of bigamy, therefore, is consummated when the second marriage was celebrated during the subsistence of the voidable first marriage. The same rule applies if the second marriage is merely considered as voidable.

To our mind, it is time to abandon the earlier precedents and adopt a more liberal view that a void ab initio marriage can be used as a defense in bigamy even without a separate judicial declaration of absolute nullity. The accused may present testimonial or documentary evidence such as the judicial declaration of absolute nullity of the first and/or subsequent void ab initio marriages in the criminal prosecution for bigamy. The said view is more in accord with the retroactive effects of a void ab initio marriage, the purpose of and legislative intent behind Article 40 of the Family Code, and the rule on statutory construction of penal laws. Therefore, the absence of a "prior valid marriage" and the subsequent judicial declaration of absolute nullity of his first marriage, Pulido is hereby acquitted from the crime of Bigamy charged against him.

WHEREFORE, the Petition for Review on Certiorari is hereby GRANTED. The March 17, 2015 Decision and August 18, 2015 Resolution of the Court of Appeals in CA-G.R. CR No. 33008 are hereby REVERSED and SET ASIDE. Petitioner Luisito G. Pulido is ACQUITTED.[^645]
* ***Alberto v. Alberto* (April 19, 2022):** Applied [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507) to reverse the Court of Appeals and reinstate the trial court's declaration of nullity; held that the husband's compulsive gambling, habitual lying, chronic financial parasitism, and complete abdication of spousal and parental responsibilities, traced to an overindulged, undisciplined childhood where his parents coddled him and tolerated his vices, and corroborated by both lay witnesses and a clinical psychologist who interviewed collateral informants, proved a grave, antecedent, and legally incurable personality structure.[^646] 
* ***Cayabyab-Navarrosa v. Navarrosa* (April 20, 2022), *Laroco v. Laroco* (June 22, 2022), *Quiogue, Jr. v. Quiogue* (August 22, 2022), *Mutya-Sumilhig v. Sumilhig* (August 22, 2022), *Republic v. Calingo* (November 23, 2022), and *Go v. Go* (December 7, 2022):** Granted nullity under [*Tan-Andal*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68507); notably, [*Quiogue, Jr. v. Quiogue*](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/1/68704) articulated the landmark doctrine on **chronic, compulsive infidelity**, holding that while isolated adultery is merely a ground for legal separation, compulsive, unremorseful serial infidelity rooted in an antecedent histrionic or narcissistic personality structure renders a spouse psychically blind to the essential marital obligation of fidelity under Article 68.[^647] [^648] [^649] [^650] [^651] [^652]

* ***Boado v. Galvez-Boado* (November 4, 2024):** Ronald, the husband, was the spouse whose incapacity supported nullity. The Supreme Court stated:

Based on the evidence presented by petitioner, he clearly and convincingly proved his psychological incapacity, specifically its juridical antecedence. It remains undisputed that petitioner grew up with a strict mother who "offered him too much demand and too little warmth." As a result, he maintained emotional distance in relationships, learning how to bottle up his true emotions and feelings just to keep the peace. This is why petitioner can perform essential marital obligations tied to providing the family's material needs, such as financial support, but lacked in providing emotional needs, the most basic of which is physical presence and companionship.

Indeed, there is evidence of the emotional distance between the spouses, which was exacerbated by the frequent physical distance between them due to their jobs. A review of the facts shows that from 1999-2002, when the parties were still dating, they were only physically together for about a year, i.e., in the years 1999-2000. From 2000 to 2002, they were apart. While they communicated over the phone, they often did so just to fight.

From the time they were married in 2002 until they separated for good in 2016, petitioner and private respondent were only physically together for about five years:

(1) from 2005-2008, when petitioner went home from Saudi Arabia;

(2) from 2011-2012, when the parties reconciled; and

(3) from 2025-2016, when petitioner went home from the United States. These show that petitioner can maintain a relationship only from a distance, frequently keeping his spouse at arm's length.

Besides, just because a spouse was once able to perform some marital obligations does not mean that they cannot be subsequently incapable of fulfilling some of the other obligations. This is why Article 36 of the Family Code states that psychological incapacity can manifest after the marriage's solemnization. In the case of petitioner, his psychological incapacity fully manifested later in the marriage, specifically in 2016, 14 years into the marriage.[^673]

Loving one's spouse is an important, if not the most important, essential marital obligation. Petitioner already asserted that he no longer loved private respondent. He also proved that his inability to love private respondent back is rooted in a durable part of his personality, caused by a potentially emotionally immature parent. For these reasons, petitioner must not be forced to stay in a loveless marriage, and his marriage to private respondent must be voided.

ACCORDINGLY, the Petition for Review on Certiorari filed by Ronald B. Boado is GRANTED. The May 12, 2022 Decision and the September 12, 2022 Resolution of the Court of Appeals in CA-G.R. CV No. 115614 are REVERSED and SET ASIDE. The Decision of Branch 34, Regional Trial Court, Balaoan, La Union in Civil Case No. 1524 is REINSTATED. The marriage between petitioner Ronald B. Boado and Florence C. Galvez-Boado is declared VOID AB INITIO due to petitioner's psychological incapacity under Article 36 of the Family Code.[^674]
